Dennis Benders, Laura Ferranti, Johannes Köhler

Abstract




Technical Report

Keywords
–Nonlinear Model Predictive Control, Time-varying Reference Tracking, Obstacle Avoidance, Terminal Ingredients, Robustness to Uncertainties, Contraction Metrics

1
Introduction

Designing a Model Predictive Control (MPC) scheme that enables a mobile robot to safely navigate through an obstacle-filled environment is a complicated yet essential task in robotics. In this technical report, safety refers to ensuring that the robot respects state and input constraints while avoiding collisions with obstacles despite the presence of disturbances and measurement noise. This report offers a step-by-step approach to implementing Nonlinear Model Predictive Control (NMPC) schemes addressing these safety requirements. Numerous books and survey papers provide comprehensive overviews of linear MPC (LMPC)
[
1
,
2
]
, NMPC
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
, and their applications in various domains, including robotics
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
. This report does not aim to replicate those exhaustive reviews. Instead, it focuses specifically on NMPC as a foundation for safe mobile robot navigation. The goal is to provide a practical and accessible path from theoretical concepts to mathematical proofs and implementation, emphasizing safety and performance guarantees. It is intended for researchers, robotics engineers, and practitioners seeking to bridge the gap between theoretical NMPC formulations and real-world robotic applications.

1.1
Overview

To implement an NMPC (hereafter referred to simply as MPC) scheme on a mobile robot, several key components must be addressed:

•

System modeling
: define the robot’s dynamics, its environment, and task objectives (Section
2
).

•

Reference tracking
: formulate the MPC problem for trajectory tracking (Section
3
). This formulation is related to the ones described in
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
.

•

Robustness to disturbances
: modify the MPC formulation to ensure safety under disturbances (Section
4
). This formulation is related to the ones described in
[
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
.

•

Robustness to model uncertainty
: develop a robust output-feedback MPC scheme to handle model uncertainty consisting of both disturbances and measurement noise (Section
5
). This formulation is related to the ones described in
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
.

Each MPC scheme is introduced in a structured manner. The formulation is presented first, followed by a discussion of its desirable properties. Detailed mathematical proofs are then provided to demonstrate how these properties are satisfied. These proofs rely on specific assumptions, which are shown to hold under the proposed designs.

1.2
Notation

Throughout this guide, vectors are denoted in bold and matrices in capital letters. The sets
{\mathbb{R}}_{[a,b)}
and
{\mathbb{N}}_{[a,b)}
represent real and natural numbers, respectively, ranging from
a
(inclusive) to
b
(exclusive). The interior of a set
A
is indicated with
\operatorname{int}(A)
. The Euclidean norm for vectors and the induced norm for matrices are denoted by
\lVert\cdot\rVert
, while the quadratic norm with respect to a positive (semi-)definite matrix
Q
is written as
\lVert{\bm{x}}\rVert_{Q}={\bm{x}}^{\top}Q{\bm{x}}
, where
Q\succ 0
or
Q\succeq 0
. Subscripts indicate time steps, indices, or both, and superscripts denote the quantity to which a variable belongs. For example,
{g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{r,o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}})
refers to the
j
th obstacle avoidance constraint for the reference trajectory, expressed in terms of the position states
{\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}}
at prediction stage
\tau
of the MPC problem solved at time
t
. The predicted state-input trajectory at time
t
is denoted by
({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}})
, with the optimal solution written as
({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*})
and a candidate solution as
({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|t}},{\tilde{\bm{u}}}_{{\tau|t}})
.

2
Mobile robot, environment, and task description

The nominal robot system dynamics are given by

{\dot{\bm{x}}}=f({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}),

(1)

with state
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
, control input
{\bm{u}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}
, and Lipschitz continuous nonlinear function
f({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})
. The system is subject to polytopic input and state constraints, also called system constraints, described as

{\mathcal{Z}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{U}}\times{\mathcal{X}}=\left\{({\bm{u}},{\bm{x}})\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}+{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}|{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}})\leq 0,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}\right\},

(2)

with
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}})={L_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{u}}\\
{\bm{x}}\end{bmatrix}-{l_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}},{L_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{n^{\mathrm{s}}}},{l_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\in{\mathbb{R}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
. Note that each input and state constraint has a lower and upper bound. Therefore, there exist
{n^{\mathrm{s}}}=2(n^{\mathrm{u}}+n^{\mathrm{x}})
system constraints in total.

The mobile robot operates in an environment with obstacles. To ensure the optimization of collision-free trajectories by the MPC, we need to define the obstacle avoidance constraints. In this guide, the obstacle avoidance constraints are assumed to be constructed as polytopic inner approximations of the possibly non-convex collision-free space. Since the trajectory of the robot progresses over time, these constraints should be defined as a function of time
t
and prediction stage
\tau
in the MPC horizon:

{\mathcal{F}}_{{\tau|t}}\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{p}}}}}\ \middle|\ {g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}})\leq 0,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}\ \tau\in[0,T],\ t\geq 0\right\},

(3)

with position
{\bm{p}}=M{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{p}}}}}
,
{g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}})={L_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}{\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}}-{l_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}},{L_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n^{\mathrm{p}}}
,
{l_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}\in\mathbb{R},

j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
. Without loss of generality, we set
\lVert{L_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}\rVert=1,j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
by scaling the constraints. For notational convenience, let’s denote the obstacle avoidance constraints by
{g^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}})={L^{\mathrm{o}}}{\bm{p}}-{l^{\mathrm{o}}}
if we write about general constraints properties and
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}})={L_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}{\bm{p}}-{l_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}},\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
if we write constraints properties that need to hold for all constraints explicitly.

Remark 1
.

Obstacle avoidance constraints (
3
) form convex polytopic sets that change over the prediction time
\tau
at time
t
. A method to compute a single convex polytope is described in
[
25
]
. Based on this,
[
14
]
leverages a sequence of polytopic obstacle avoidance constraints, which are used in this work. A similar way to represent the obstacle avoidance constraints is recently proposed in
[
26
]
, in which the constraints are continuously parameterized and differentiable off-centered ellipsoids based on polynomial path segments. This allows them to be more flexible at the cost of increased computation time compared to (
3
).

The task of the mobile robot is to track a reference trajectory that satisfies the following property:

Property 1
.

Reference trajectory

{\bm{r}}=[{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}^{\top}{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}^{\top}]^{\top}:

(4)

1.

is dynamically feasible:

{\dot{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{r}}}_{{t+\tau}}=f({\bm{x}}_{{t+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}},{\bm{u}}_{{t+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}});

(5)

2.

satisfies a tightened set of the system constraints:

{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}}\in{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}},

(6)

where
{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}
is defined as

{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}\coloneqq{\bar{{\mathcal{U}}}}\times{\bar{{\mathcal{X}}}}=\left\{({{\bm{u}},{\bm{x}}})\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}+{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\ \middle|\ {g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})\leq 0,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{int}({\mathcal{Z}}),

(7)

with later introduced functions
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}}),\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
;

3.

satisfies a tightened set of the obstacle avoidance constraints:

M{\bm{x}}_{{t+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\in\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\tau|t}},

(8)

where
\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\tau|t}}
is defined as

\bar{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\tau|t}}\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{p}}}}}\ \middle|\ {g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{r,o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}})\leq 0,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{int}({\mathcal{F}}_{{\tau|t}}),\ \tau\in[0,T],\ t\geq 0,

(9)

with later introduced functions
{g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{r,o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}}),\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
;

The tightening of the system and obstacle avoidance constraints is included to ensure that the original constraints are satisfied as long as the real robot tracks the reference trajectory with a bounded error.

In general, generating references trajectories satisfying Property
1
is non-trivial. Therefore, a method to generate such reference trajectories is proposed in
[
14
]
.

3
MPC for trajectory tracking

This section aims to introduce the MPC scheme that will be used to track the reference trajectory defined in Property
1
, the so-called tracking MPC (TMPC). To explain TMPC’s properties, Section
3.1
directly states its formulation. Then, we will discuss the elements in the formulation and the properties it should satisfy to ensure that the mobile robot safely navigates through the environment. This is the topic of Section
3.2
. After discussing the TMPC design, we will provide some concluding remarks on the TMPC formulation in Section
3.3
.

3.1
TMPC formulation

Consider the following MPC formulation for tracking a reference trajectory defined in Property
1
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bm{x}}_{{\cdot|t}},{\bm{u}}_{{\cdot|t}}\end{subarray}}{{\operatorname{min}}}\ \

\displaystyle\mathrlap{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})+\int_{0}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})\ d\tau,}

(10a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{{0|t}}={\bm{x}}_{t},

(10b)

\displaystyle{\dot{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|t}}=f({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}),

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(10c)

\displaystyle{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}})\leq 0,

\displaystyle\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(10d)

\displaystyle{g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{\tau|t}})\leq 0,

\displaystyle\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(10e)

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}\in{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}),

(10f)

with stage cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{r}})=\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\bm{u}}-{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{R}^{2},Q\succ 0,R\succ 0
and terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})=\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{P}^{2},P\succ 0
. In this formulation,
Q
and
R
are tuning matrices, and
P
should be suitably computed to ensure stability as given in Section
3.2.3
. The optimal solution to (
10
) is denoted by
{\bm{x}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*}
and
{\bm{u}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*}
, with
{\bm{x}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*}
being the optimal state trajectory and
{\bm{u}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*}
being the optimal control input trajectory.

TMPC problem (
10
) is applied in a receding-horizon fashion, meaning that it is solved every
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
seconds and the optimal control input
{\bm{u}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*}
is applied during each interval with length
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
. According to this principle, the control law for the closed-loop system is defined as follows for
t=i{T^{\mathrm{s}}},i\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

{\bm{u}}_{t+\tau}={\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},\ \tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}].

(11)

3.2
TMPC properties

The advantage of running a TMPC on a mobile robot is that if (
10
) can be solved, it is the optimal solution to the problem. The optimal solution is a feasible solution to (
10
), i.e., one that satisfies all constraints (
10b
)-(
10f
). Furthermore, it minimizes objective (
10a
). If the problem is suitably formulated, the robot will efficiently navigate through the environment by accurately tracking the reference trajectory.

Note that MPC is a trajectory optimization approach. Theoretically, one would like to optimize a suitable trajectory for all future times. However, this is not a tractable problem to solve. Therefore, the MPC horizon is finite. To ensure that using a finite horizon does not lead to unavoidable infeasible problems in the future, so-called terminal ingredients are designed
[
2
]
. The terminal ingredients include the terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}}
, terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}
, and terminal set
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}
. The idea behind the terminal ingredients is that one could safely execute the open-loop MPC strategy appended with
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}}
. This might not be the optimal behavior since (
10
) is not solved every
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
seconds, but it would be feasible. If designed suitably, the execution of
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}}
after
{\bm{u}}_{[0,T]|t}^{*}
renders
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}
an upper bound for the ‘infinite-horizon’ cost that is not accounted for in the integral term of (
10a
). Furthermore, if
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}
is suitably designed, this set is positive invariant under
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}}
. This means that the system is guaranteed to remain within this set. As one can imagine, the terminal ingredients are crucial for the safety guarantees of an MPC scheme. Therefore, they are central to the MPC design.

The discussion on terminal ingredients above holds for classical stabilization MPC types that bring the system to an equilibrium state. Recently, this theory has been extended to tracking dynamic reference trajectories, such as the one defined in Property
1
, rendering them useful for mobile robot deployment. As a result, the terminal ingredients all have (
4
) as input argument. In particular, their commonly used expressions are

{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}={\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}+K({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}),

(12)

for the terminal control law,

{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})=\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{P}^{2},

(13)

for the terminal cost and

{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})=\left\{{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\ \middle|\ {{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})\leq\alpha^{2}\right\},

(14)

for the terminal set. Thus,
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
consists of a feed-forward reference input term and feedback gain matrix
K\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
, correcting the error between the reference state
{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}
and real state
{\bm{x}}
. Furthermore,
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
is a sublevel set of the terminal cost with scaling
\alpha>0
.

Remark 2
.

In general, feedback gain matrix
K
can be state-dependent or time-dependent; see
[
13
]
and the discussion therein. For the sake of simplicity, we assume that
K
is constant in this guide.

Important to note is that one does not implement
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
. Instead, the MPC scheme is implemented in a receding-horizon fashion as given by (
11
). This means that the control action is recomputed at times
t=i{T^{\mathrm{s}}},i\in{\mathbb{N}}
and applied during intervals
[i{T^{\mathrm{s}}},(i+1){T^{\mathrm{s}}}),i\in{\mathbb{N}}
. A problem that could arise in practice is that TMPC problem (
10
) becomes infeasible at some point. For example, this happens when the solver experiences numerical problems. This means the solver cannot find a solution satisfying all constraints. In this case, the robot has no known control action to take. The TMPC formulation should satisfy the recursive feasibility property to prevent this issue. This property ensures that, given that a feasible solution to (
10
) is found at time
t
, a feasible solution can also be constructed for time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
without solving the optimization problem, such that the constraints in (
10
) are satisfied at all future times. Recursive feasibility is considered a necessary condition to ensure safe mobile robot deployment.

Remark 3
.

In dynamic environments, the constraints might vary over time. This could break the recursive feasibility property since the previously optimal solution appended by
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
does not necessarily satisfy the updated constraints. This does not mean the receding-horizon MPC implementation will not find an optimal solution at each time step. This means that we cannot guarantee that a feasible solution will always exist. Therefore, to prove safety in such environments, one must make an assumption about the environment. For example, as long as the robot’s trajectory can be predicted well, the moving obstacles will avoid the robot. Alternatively, and possibly more standard, is the assumption that there exist known sets that over-approximate the dynamic obstacles over time
[
16
]
. The latter assumption is usually limited to structured environments with predictable object movements or results in conservative plans. Less conservative motion plans are constructed in more commonly adopted approaches such as stochastic MPC, e.g.,
[
27
]
. These approaches plan safe trajectories in dynamic environments up to a certain risk level based on future object trajectory predictions. However, the level of safety guarantees decreases for these schemes.

Given the receding-horizon implementation of TMPC, it is also important to show that, even though the predicted state might come closer to the reference trajectory over the prediction horizon, the closed-loop robot state converges to the reference trajectory. We will call this the trajectory tracking property.

Important to note is that the existence of the above-specified terminal ingredients is not guaranteed in general. We will show later how to design them. For now, we assume their existence. This gives the following assumption:

Assumption 1

(Terminal ingredients)
.

There exist control law (
12
) with feedback matrix
K\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
, terminal cost (
13
) with terminal cost matrix
P\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
, and terminal set (
14
) with terminal set scaling
\alpha>0
, such that the following properties hold for all
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
:

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})\leq-\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau,

(15a)

\displaystyle\left({\bm{x}},{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}\right)\in{\mathcal{Z}},

(15b)

\displaystyle M{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{F}},

(15c)

with sampling time
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}>0
, optimal solution
({\bm{x}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*})
at
t
for all
t\geq 0
and its extended version using terminal control law (
12
)
{\bm{x}}_{{T+\tau}|t}^{*}
for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, as defined in Section
3.2.1
.

This assumption is used to complete the trajectory tracking and recursive feasibility proofs in Sections
3.2.3
and
3.2.2
. The meaning of the quantities in this assumption will become clearer in the following sections.

Figure 1:
Visualization of a 1D reference trajectory
{\bm{x}}_{t+[0,4{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]}^{\mathrm{r}}
, previously optimal solution
{\bm{x}}_{[0,T]|t}^{*}
, appended part of previously optimal solution
{\bm{x}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
, candidate solution
{\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{[0,T]|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
, terminal set
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}^{*},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})\leq\alpha^{2}
corresponding to
{\bm{x}}_{[0,T]|t}^{*}
, and terminal set
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})\leq\alpha^{2}
corresponding to candidate solution. In this case we set
T=4{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
. Note that the terminal sets are visualized as ellipses to clarify their shapes, whereas they should be visualized as vertical lines in this case.

To provide some intuition behind the proofs beforehand, Figure
1
shows the optimal solution at time
t
and the corresponding candidate solution at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. Thus, the candidate solution covers the prediction horizon of the previously optimal solution shifted by
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
. The idea behind the recursive feasibility proof is to show that the candidate satisfies all constraints (
10b
)-(
10f
) at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. In interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝, these constraints are trivially satisfied since the candidate overlaps with the optimal solution at time
t
. The main thing to show is that once terminal set (
14
) is reached at the end of the prediction horizon at
t
, ensured by (
10f
), we can append control law (
12
) to the previously optimal solution to obtain a candidate solution that satisfies system and obstacle avoidance constraints (
10d
) and (
10e
), respectively, and terminal set constraint (
10f
). A trivial method to prove recursive feasibility is to show that control law (
12
) renders (
14
) invariant. This guarantees that the candidate satisfies (
10f
). Furthermore, by (
15b
) and (
15c
) in Assumption
1
, (
10d
) and (
10e
) are also satisfied in terminal set (
14
).

Given that the candidate is a feasible solution to (
10
) at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
, the trajectory tracking proof entails showing that cost (
10a
) is lower for the candidate than for the previously optimal solution, meaning that the robot will converge to the reference trajectory even though (
10
) is not solved again at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. Intuitively, this holds if the cost related to interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝, shortly cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝, is lower than terminal cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{3}⃝ such that the candidate cost decreases at least by cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{1}⃝ compared to the cost of the previously optimal solution since cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝ remains the same. This is exactly the condition that is written mathematically in (
15a
) in Assumption
1
.

In summary, ensuring that TMPC formulation (
10
) leads to safe and efficient mobile robot deployment requires the design of suitable terminal ingredients. To this end, we define the candidate solution based on the terminal ingredients in Section
3.2.1
that allows us to prove recursive feasibility in Section
3.2.2
and trajectory tracking in Section
3.2.3
. As will become clear, Assumption
1
is not the only assumption to satisfy to complete the proofs. Therefore, the goal of Section
3.2.4
is to design the involved quantities so that all assumptions are satisfied and the desired TMPC properties hold.

3.2.1
Candidate solution

The goal of defining the candidate solution is to show that there exists a feasible, not necessarily optimal, solution to (
10
) at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
given that (
10
) is successfully solved at time
t
. The trivial way to construct such a candidate is to pick the overlapping part with the optimal solution from time
t
, i.e., the part in prediction interval
\tau\in[{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at
t
, since this trajectory is known to have satisfied all constraints in the MPC run at time
t
and overlaps with
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. What is left is to define the candidate for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
. This trajectory part satisfies the constraints if we find
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
with the abovementioned properties.

Let’s now mathematically define the candidate. For convenience of notation, we define the appended part of the previously optimal solution, for
\tau\in[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, as

{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}={\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}}),

(16)

with
{\dot{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}
given by (
1
) by applying (
16
).

Given these definitions, we can write the candidate for time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
as

{\tilde{\bm{u}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}={\bm{u}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},\ {\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}={\bm{x}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},\ \tau\in[0,T].

(17)

The candidate, and thus
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
, is not necessarily implemented in practice. It is only used to prove recursive feasibility and trajectory tracking. One would need this control law if the problem runs infeasible, and one wants the robot to continue executing the open-loop MPC prediction during deployment. However, in that situation, the control action no longer considers any changes in the robot’s state or environment, which can be dangerous. In that case, it would be better to stop, check the log to see why the problem was infeasible, and fix it.

Remark 4
.

Since the candidate gives the desired recursive feasibility and trajectory tracking properties as shown in the next sections, it would be sufficient to only solve (
10
) at
t=0
and apply the candidate for
t>0
. This reasoning conforms to the arguments presented in
[
28
]
: feasibility implies stability. However, the resulting control policy is sub-optimal regarding the defined objective function. Therefore, the user can explicitly trade optimality and computational efficiency.

Although the candidate is not necessarily implemented in practice, it is a suitable warm start for the solver since it is a feasible solution that might be close to the optimal solution. Providing a proper warm start to the solver is vital to speeding up the solve time, as the solver needs fewer iterations to go from the initial point to the optimal solution.

3.2.2
Recursive feasibility proof

To prove recursive feasibility, our task is to show that candidate (
17
) satisfies all constraints (
10b
)-(
10f
) at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. First, note that, without knowing anything about the terminal ingredients, the candidate solution implies that constraint (
10b
) is satisfied and constraint (
10c
) is satisfied for
\tau\in[0,T]
. That leaves us to show that constraints (
10d
) and (
10e
) are satisfied for
\tau\in[0,T]
and that the terminal set constraint (
10f
) is satisfied at
\tau=T
.

To show that constraints (
10d
) and (
10e
) are satisfied for
\tau\in[0,T]
, we split this interval up into the half-open interval
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
and the closed interval
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
.

System constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})}

Let’s start with the proofs for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
. For this interval, the following holds for the system constraints
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{u}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_candidate}}{=}{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_sys}}{\leq}0.

(18)

Obstacle avoidance constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})}

Similarly, the obstacle avoidance constraints
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
are satisfied for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
:

{g_{j,{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\tilde{\bm{p}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_candidate}}{=}{g_{j,{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_obs}}{\leq}0.

(19)

Note that, in contrast to the system constraints, the obstacle avoidance constraints are time-varying. Therefore, we need the following assumption on the obstacle avoidance constraints in order to show that
{g_{j,{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})\leq 0
actually holds:

Assumption 2

(Obstacle avoidance constraints)
.

The obstacle avoidance constraints should satisfy

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},\ \tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}],

(20a)

\displaystyle{\bm{p}}_{{T|t}}^{*}

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{T|t}}.

(20b)

In words, the previously optimal path
{\bm{p}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}
should be contained in the corresponding constraint regions
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}
applied for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. Similarly, the terminal position
{\bm{p}}_{{T|t}}^{*}
should be contained in the terminal constraint region
{\mathcal{F}}_{{T|t}}
. The latter property ensures that the appended part of the candidate solution, given by (
16
), satisfies the updated obstacle avoidance constraints at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. This property is used to show terminal constraint satisfaction below. We assume the ‘corresponding’ constraint regions are constructed using the previously optimal solution.

Terminal set constraint satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]}

Next, we prove that if (
10f
) is satisfied at time
t
, the candidate (
17
) satisfies (
10f
) in prediction interval
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
for time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. For this proof, we leverage the assumed decrease in
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
, given by (
15a
) in Assumption
1
. Correspondingly, the following holds for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
:

{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_candidate}}{=}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_ass_term_jf}}{\leq}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})\overset{\eqref{eq:tmpc_term}\eqref{eq:tmpc_xf}}{\leq}\alpha^{2}.

(21)

System constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]}

Satisfaction of system constraints (
10d
) in prediction interval
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
is a direct consequence of the combination of terminal set invariance, as shown in the previous section, and invoking (
15b
) in Assumption
1
.

Obstacle avoidance constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]}

Similar to the system constraints satisfaction, satisfaction of obstacle avoidance constraints (
10e
) in prediction interval
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
is a direct consequence of the combination of terminal set invariance, as shown in the previous section, and invoking (
15c
) in Assumption
1
.
∎

3.2.3
Trajectory tracking proof

Similar to proving the stability of an autonomous system, proving that nominal system (
1
) tracks reference trajectory (
4
) under closed-loop control law (
11
) follows arguments from Lyapunov theory. A useful analogy to Lyapunov’s theory is the energy captured in a mechanical system. In this analogy, the Lyapunov function describes the energy stored in the system. In such a system, the energy decreases over time because of dissipation effects like friction, meaning that the Lyapunov function value will strictly decrease over time. In the context of MPC, the energy is based on the tracking error, and the dissipation effect that reduces the tracking error is the closed-loop control law (
11
). Thus, a logical choice for the Lyapunov function is to pick the optimal cost
{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})
in (
10
) since this cost indicates how close the predicted trajectory is to the reference trajectory. Ideally, we would like the optimal cost to decrease over time, meaning the tracking error converges to zero.

To derive a bound on the optimal cost, we follow similar steps to the standard descent proof for the candidate cost in
[
3
]
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}})

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{u}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})d\tau+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

(22a)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{u}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{u}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})d\tau+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

(22b)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(c)}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau

\displaystyle+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

(22c)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

(22d)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(e)}}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

(22e)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(f)}}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{x}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau

(22f)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(g)}}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau+{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau

(22g)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(h)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau,

(22h)

where (a) is obtained by filling in (
10a
) with the feasible, but not necessarily optimal candidate (
17
), the so-called candidate cost, (b) by splitting the integral term at
T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
, (c) by filling in candidate (
17
), (d) by adding and subtracting
\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau
, (e) by replacing
\int_{0}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau
with
{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})
, (f) by conveniently re-arranging the terms, (g) by (
17
), and (h) by (
15a
) in Assumption
1
.

In simple words, (
15a
) in Assumption
1
states that the added candidate cost in prediction interval
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
should be less than the terminal predicted cost at predicted time
T
at
t
. If this is the case, the candidate cost
{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}})
is at least the stage cost for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
at
t
smaller than the previously optimal cost
{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})
. Thus, even without optimizing (
10
) at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
and instead applying candidate (
17
), the cost strictly decreases. Given that the optimal cost
{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}})
is upper-bounded by the candidate cost, the optimal cost also strictly decreases.

Thus, (
15a
) in Assumption
1
implies

{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}})-{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})\leq-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau.

(23)

It is not trivial to find a
{\mathrm{class\text{-}}{\mathcal{K}}_{\infty}}
function that forms a lower bound to
\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau
, which would allow us to show that
\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{u}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau>0
. Hence, we cannot prove stability utilizing standard Lyapunov arguments outlined in
[
3
]
. As a convergence proof does not require this bound, we prove convergence instead.

The descent property of the optimal cost (
23
) implies

{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}})-{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})\leq-c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau,

(24)

with constant
c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}>0
. Reversing the sign in (
24
), iterating this inequality from
0
to
t
with
t\rightarrow\infty
, and using the fact that
{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})
is uniformly bounded yields

\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{lim}}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau\leq{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{0},{\bm{r}}_{0})-\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{lim}}{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})\leq{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{0},{\bm{r}}_{0})<\infty.

(25)

By applying Barbalat’s lemma
[
29
]
, we can conclude that the tracking error
\lVert{\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert
asymptotically converges to zero. ∎

3.2.4
Design to satisfy the assumptions

This section will detail the design of:

•

the obstacle avoidance constraints, which need to satisfy Assumption
2
;

•

the terminal ingredients, which need to satisfy (
15a
) in Assumption
1
;

•

the reference trajectory design, which needs to satisfy (
15b
) and (
15c
) in Assumption
1
.

Obstacle avoidance constraints design

To prove recursive feasibility, we need the obstacle avoidance constraints to satisfy Assumption
2
. A trivial way to satisfy this assumption is to generate the obstacle avoidance constraints based on the previously optimal path
{\bm{p}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*}
and ensure that this path is also contained in the corresponding constraint regions.

Remark 5
.

Since TMPC (
10
) will be implemented using multiple shooting with discretized dynamics
[
3
]
, we also define the obstacle avoidance constraints based on piecewise-constant segments between MPC stages. This means that the obstacle avoidance constraints are also piecewise defined according to

{\mathcal{F}}_{i{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}={\mathcal{F}}_{i{T^{\mathrm{s}}}|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},\ \tau\in(0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}],\ i\in\mathbb{N}_{[0,N-1]},

(26)

where
N
is the number of discrete predicted stages.

Terminal ingredients design

The terminal ingredients design involves computing a suitable combination of terminal control law (
12
), terminal cost
13
, and terminal set (
14
) that satisfy terminal cost decrease (
15a
) in Assumption
1
. This design involves finding a suitable combination of matrices
P
and
K
and constant
\alpha>0
.

To design
P
and
K
, recent work
[
13
]
proposes a linear matrix inequality (LMI) to ensure direct satisfaction of Assumption
1
:

\left(A({\bm{r}})+B({\bm{r}})K({\bm{r}})\right)^{\top}P({\bm{r}})+P({\bm{r}})\left(A({\bm{r}})+B({\bm{r}})K({\bm{r}})\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}+{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}\frac{\partial P({\bm{r}})}{\partial{\bm{x}}}{\dot{\bm{x}}}_{j}+\left({Q^{\epsilon}}+K({\bm{r}})RK({\bm{r}})\right)\leq 0,

(27)

with Jacobians

A({\bm{r}})\coloneqq\left.\frac{\partial f({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})}{\partial{\bm{x}}}\right|_{{\bm{r}}},\quad B({\bm{r}})\coloneqq\left.\frac{\partial f({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})}{\partial{\bm{u}}}\right|_{{\bm{r}}},

(28)

{Q^{\epsilon}}\coloneqq Q+\epsilon{I^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
, and
\epsilon>0
. See
[
13
]
for the corresponding proof.

In particular, this LMI ensures a system property called local incremental stabilizability. See
[
30
,
31
]
for more details. Note that
P
and
K
are parameterized based on local values
{\bm{r}}
in the state-input space of the reference trajectory. This is where the word ‘local’ comes from. It suffices to show that certain properties hold locally in the state-input space to ensure tracking of reference trajectories in the complete state-input space. However, note that the results only hold for sufficiently small values of terminal set scaling
\alpha
such that Jacobians (
28
) are accurate enough to describe the nonlinear system behavior. The word ‘incremental’ refers to the fact that we consider the stabilizability property of the system trajectory that evolves close to the reference trajectory.

As mentioned before, terminal cost (
13
) is an upper bound of the ‘infinite-horizon’ cost that we would ideally like to minimize. Therefore, the goal is to find the minimum-valued matrix
P({\bm{r}})
and corresponding matrix
K({\bm{r}})
satisfying LMI (
27
). Correspondingly, we can formulate the following semi-definite program (SDP):

\displaystyle\underset{X,Y}{{\operatorname{min}}}\ \

\displaystyle-\operatorname{log}\operatorname{det}{X_{\mathrm{min}}},

(29a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle X({\bm{r}})\succeq 0,

(29b)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}A({\bm{r}})X({\bm{r}})+B({\bm{r}})Y({\bm{r}})+\left(A({\bm{r}})X({\bm{r}})+B({\bm{r}})Y({\bm{r}})\right)^{\top}-\dot{X}({\bm{r}})&\left({Q^{\epsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}X({\bm{r}})\right)^{\top}&\left(R^{\frac{1}{2}}Y({\bm{r}})\right)^{\top}\\
{Q^{\epsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}}X({\bm{r}})&-{I^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}&0^{{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}\\
R^{\frac{1}{2}}Y({\bm{r}})&0^{{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}&-{I^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}\end{bmatrix}\preceq 0,

(29c)

\displaystyle{X_{\mathrm{min}}}\preceq X({\bm{r}}),\ \forall{\bm{r}}\in{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}},

with
\dot{X}({\bm{r}})\coloneqq\frac{\partial X({\bm{r}})}{\partial{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}}{\dot{\bm{x}}^{\mathrm{r}}}
and LMI (
29c
) being the direct result of taking the Schur complement of LMI (
27
) and applying the following coordinate transform:

P({\bm{r}})=X({\bm{r}})^{-1},\ K({\bm{r}})=Y({\bm{r}})P({\bm{r}}).

(30)

Note that
{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}
represents the continuous state-input space of the reference trajectory. Therefore, SDP (
29
) is semi-infinite, and we need to grid or convexify
{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}
to solve it. For solving the SDP, the states and inputs in
{\bm{r}}
that do not appear in the Jacobians (
28
) can be set to zero, we can take the vertices for the ones that appear linearly, and the ones that appear nonlinearly need to be gridded.

Remark 6
.

Since the computation time of generating LMI (
29c
) can become significantly large, a solution is to solve (
29
) with a coarse grid of points and evaluate the result at a denser grid to check the validity of the result.

Given this method to compute
P({\bm{r}})
and
K({\bm{r}})
, the only thing left is to design a suitable value for terminal set scaling
\alpha
. Theoretically, one could set
\alpha=0
, such that (
10f
) becomes an equality constraint. However, this unnecessarily restricts the solver from finding a solution. This might lead to infeasibility, either because a solution satisfying this equality constraint is non-existent or because the problem is numerically infeasible. Therefore, the idea is to find a non-conservative maximum value for
\alpha
. Recall that (
14
) is centered around the reference trajectory, and the reference trajectory satisfies tightened system and obstacle avoidance constraints (
7
) and (
9
), respectively. Given this information,
\alpha
can only grow as large as the tightening applied to the original system and obstacle avoidance constraints.

Let’s assume the reference trajectory is only subject to tightened system constraints and is constrained by the following polytopic set:
{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}=\left\{{\bm{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}+{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\ \middle|\ {L_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}{\bm{r}}\leq{l_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}},\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}\right\}
. The tightening equals
{l_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}-{L_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}{\bm{r}},{\bm{r}}\in{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
. As a result,
\alpha
can be computed by solving the following linear program (LP)
[
13
]
, which is proven in Appendix
A
:

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}=

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha}{{\operatorname{max}}}\

\displaystyle\alpha,

(31a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle\left\lVert P({\bm{r}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[K({\bm{r}})^{\top}{I^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\right]{L_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}^{\top}\right\rVert\alpha\leq({l_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}-{L_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}{\bm{r}}),

(31b)

\displaystyle\forall{\bm{r}}\in{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}},\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}.

(31c)

Naturally, this result can also be extended to include obstacle avoidance constraints.

Note that using this expression, we can either compute the value of
\alpha
based on the tightening of the system constraints for the reference trajectory or vice versa. Therefore, we need more details on the system and obstacle avoidance constraints before finishing the design of
\alpha
. This will be described in the next section.

Reference trajectory design

The reference trajectory needs to satisfy tightened system constraints (
7
) and tightened obstacle avoidance constraints (
9
). These constraint sets need to be constructed such that the system and obstacle avoidance constraints are satisfied in terminal set (
14
) according to (
15b
) and (
15c
) in Assumption
1
.

Given the fact that system constraints (
2
) are continuously differentiable and defined on compact set
{\mathcal{Z}}
, they are Lipschitz continuous. Therefore, we can express them as

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}}^{\prime},{\bm{u}}^{\prime})-{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})\leq{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\alpha,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

(32)

for some
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\neq{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
and
{\bm{u}}^{\prime}\neq{\bm{u}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}
with Lipschitz constants
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
. As a result, if we set the tightened system constraints of the reference trajectory as

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})\leq{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\alpha,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

(33)

we get a strict equality in (
31b
) with

{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}=\left\lVert P({\bm{r}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[K({\bm{r}})^{\top}{I^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\right]{L_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}^{\top}\right\rVert,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}.

(34)

Equivalently, if we set the upper bound of the tightened obstacle avoidance constraints of the reference trajectory as

{g^{\mathrm{r,o}}}({\bm{p}})\leq{g^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\alpha,

(35)

we can replace
\left[K({\bm{r}})^{\top}{I^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\right]{L_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}^{\top}
by
M^{\top}
to obtain the following expression for
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}
:

{c^{\mathrm{o}}}=\left\lVert P({\bm{r}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}M^{\top}\right\rVert.

(36)

Given this design, we choose a value for
\alpha
that results in a desired tightening of either of the system constraints or the obstacle avoidance constraints. A natural choice for mobile robots is to decide on the maximum distance between reference trajectory and obstacles. Let’s call this quantity
{d_{\mathrm{max}}}
. Then, we know that
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\alpha={d_{\mathrm{max}}}
, which results in the following expression for the terminal set scaling:

\alpha=\frac{{d_{\mathrm{max}}}}{{c^{\mathrm{o}}}}.

(37)

By solving (
29
) some system constraints might be tightened more than others, leading to empty feasible sets. This issue can be avoided by including the system constraints tightening constants in (
29a
) and setting their penalties equal to the inverse of the space in the corresponding system constraint direction
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
. This way, the solver is encouraged to tighten the system constraints equally. Working out Lipschitz inequality on the system constraints (
33
) using
\alpha=\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{P({\bm{r}})}
and taking the Schur complement according to
[
32
, p. 29]
results in the following LMI:

\begin{bmatrix}{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}^{2}&L_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}\begin{bmatrix}Y\\
X\end{bmatrix}\\
\left(L_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}\begin{bmatrix}Y\\
X\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\top}&X\end{bmatrix}\succeq 0,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}.

(38)

This LMI is included in the SDP formulation in
[
14
]
. In that formulation, the state- and input-dependency of
P
and
K
are also removed, so the results hold in the complete state-input space, not just locally. Therefore, the constant
\epsilon
from
[
13
]
, that accounts for the Lipschitz continuity of Jacobians (
28
), can be removed. The advantage of including the state- and input-dependency is the reduced conservatism, i.e., the terminal cost is smaller and the terminal set is larger. This results in a smaller tracking error of the closed-loop system. However, the increased conservatism was not a major concern in
[
14
]
and resulted in a simpler formulation.

3.3
Concluding remarks

In conclusion, the presented TMPC scheme is an effective tool for providing safety guarantees for tracking dynamically feasible reference trajectories with a mobile robot described by nominal dynamics (
1
). The scheme is based on the idea that suitably designed terminal ingredients result in a strict decrease in terminal cost and positive invariance of the terminal set, thereby allowing for proving trajectory tracking and recursive feasibility of the scheme.

Note that the solver will only find a feasible solution to (
10
) if the system starts sufficiently close to the reference trajectory such that terminal set constraint (
10f
) is satisfied.

In practice, a physical robot is subject to dynamical disturbances that could be impossible to model. To ensure safe mobile robot navigation despite these disturbances, we need to know how big the effects of the disturbances on the dynamics are and account for that in the MPC design. This is the topic of Section
4
in which the disturbed robot dynamics and a corresponding robust MPC (RMPC) design are provided.

4
Robust MPC for trajectory tracking with disturbances

The goal of this section is to introduce the RMPC scheme that will be used to track reference trajectories satisfying Property
1
when the system is subject to disturbances. The description of the system dynamics is provided in Section
4.1
. Like Section
3.1
, Section
4.2
states the optimization problem and closed-loop control law. Then, Section
4.3
builds intuition for RMPC and explains the required elements in the formulation and related properties. Finally, Section
4.4
concludes the RMPC formulation.

4.1
Disturbed mobile robot description

The disturbed system dynamics are given by

{\dot{\bm{x}}}={f^{\mathrm{w}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{w}})}\coloneqq f({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}})+E{\bm{w}},

(39)

with the same properties as the nominal system (
1
), disturbance
{\bm{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{w}}}}}
, and disturbance selection matrix
E\in\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{w}}}}
. From now on, to make a distinction between the nominal and disturbed system, let’s denote nominal and disturbed states by
{\bm{z}}
and
{\bm{x}}
, respectively.

By recording data one can compute a bounding box with lower bound
{\underaccent{\bar}{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}
and upper bound
{\bar{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}
for each dimension
i\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{w}}}]}}
of the recorded disturbance values. Let’s denote the offset for each dimension
i
by
{\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}\coloneqq\frac{{\underaccent{\bar}{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}+{\bar{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}}{2}
. Then, the disturbance set centered around
\bm{0}
is given by

{\mathcal{W}}\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{w}}}}}\ \middle|\ {\underaccent{\bar}{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec,0}}\leq{\bm{w}}_{i}\leq{\bar{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec,0}},\ i\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{w}}}]}}\right\},

(40)

with
{\underaccent{\bar}{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec,0}}\coloneqq{\underaccent{\bar}{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}-{\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}
and
{\bar{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec,0}}\coloneqq{\bar{\bm{w}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}-{\bm{w}}_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}
, such that the recorded disturbance values are all contained in set
{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}\oplus{\mathcal{W}}
. Correspondingly, we write the disturbance values in the next sections as

{\bm{w}}\coloneqq{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}+{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}\oplus{\mathcal{W}}.

(41)

Model bias
{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
can be included in the nominal prediction model to account for the static offset of disturbances as described in the next section.

4.2
RMPC formulation

Consider the following RMPC formulation for tracking reference trajectory (
4
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bm{z}}_{{\cdot|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\cdot|t}}\end{subarray}}{{\operatorname{min}}}\ \

\displaystyle\mathrlap{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})+\int_{0}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})\ d\tau,}

(42a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle{\bm{z}}_{{0|t}}={\bm{x}}_{t},

(42b)

\displaystyle s_{\tau}=0,

(42c)

\displaystyle{\dot{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|t}}=f({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(42d)

\displaystyle{\dot{s}}_{\tau}=-\rho s_{\tau}+{\bar{w}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(42e)

\displaystyle{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}\leq 0,

\displaystyle\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(42f)

\displaystyle{g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}s_{\tau}\leq 0,

\displaystyle\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(42g)

\displaystyle({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}},s_{\tau})\in{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}),

(42h)

with stage cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{v}},{\bm{r}})=\lVert{\bm{z}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\bm{v}}-{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{R}^{2},Q\succ 0,R\succ 0
and terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})=\lVert{\bm{z}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{P}^{2},P\succ 0
. In this formulation,
Q
and
R
are tuning matrices and
P
should be suitably computed to show practical asymptotic convergence, see Section
4.3.3
. Note the inclusion of model bias
{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
in nominal system dynamics (
42d
) such that the model best describes the minimum and maximum disturbance bounds.

Similar to the TMPC formulation in Section
3
, the RMPC formulation is applied in a receding-horizon fashion. In this case, the control law for the closed-loop system is defined as follows for
t\geq 0
:

{\bm{u}}_{{t+\tau}}=\kappa({\bm{x}}_{{t+\tau}},{\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*})\coloneqq{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}+{\kappa^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+\tau}},{\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}),\ \tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}],

(43)

with control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}:{{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}}
including feedback law
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}:{{{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}}
that will be defined later according to the design presented in this section.

4.3
RMPC properties

If the nominal system (
1
) is subject to a predictable disturbance, we could adjust (
1
) to incorporate the corresponding disturbance dynamics and apply the TMPC described in the previous section. However, in general, disturbances are not or only partially predictable. The predictable part could be learned with a learning-based method such as Gaussian Processes, see for example
[
33
,
34
,
35
]
. However, in almost all cases, there will be a part of the dynamics that cannot be predicted. In that case, we still need to be able to guarantee that operating our MPC scheme on the robot is safe.

Then why not execute the TMPC from Section
3
and hope for the best? Let’s consider the scenario where the reference trajectory is computed using the same constraints as the TMPC. At some time
t
, the state might exactly overlap with the reference state, i.e.,
{\bm{x}}_{t}={\bm{x}}_{t}^{\mathrm{r}}
. However, the worst-case disturbance happens during interval
[t,{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}]
. This causes the state to exceed the constraint boundary. Suddenly, the problem has become infeasible, and there is no structured way to recover, i.e., there does not exist a suitable candidate solution that could be applied in an open loop. From the analysis above, we can conclude that if the reference trajectory is generated far enough away from the constraints, i.e., the tightening
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\alpha,j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
or
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\alpha
is sufficiently large, we can prevent the occurrence of this problem. This is the exact reason why the experiments on the real robot using the TMPC scheme presented in
[
14
]
were successful despite explicit robust design: the distance
{d_{\mathrm{max}}}
to the obstacle avoidance constraints was tuned sufficiently well to avoid the obstacles and make sure the system constraints were satisfied at all times in the presence of disturbances.

This is not the complete story, though. Consider the scenario where the reference trajectory is generated far enough from the constraint boundaries. And let’s again consider the positive scenario where
{\bm{x}}_{t}={\bm{x}}_{t}^{\mathrm{r}}
. At
t
, the TMPC would make a nominal prediction according to dynamics (
1
) and execute the control law in a closed loop. However, during interval
[t,t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, the worst-case disturbance impacts the system. So, instead of continuing to track the reference, the state moves away from the reference. The TMPC did not anticipate this. At time
t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
, the TMPC optimizes a nominal predicted trajectory again from the last received sample of the disturbed state. By executing the optimal input during the interval
[t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}},t+2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, the system will move towards the reference trajectory again. Thus, even without explicitly considering disturbances, the TMPC might be able to track (
4
). Of course, the tracking is not perfect, but at least the system stays close to the reference trajectory. This feedback property of TMPC is called inherent robustness
[
36
]
. It is a property that most practical MPC implementations rely on, and that might be sufficient to track the reference trajectory.

Inherent robustness alone might not be sufficient to ensure the robot’s safety. For example, another disturbance may impact the system during
[t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}},t+2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
and, despite the optimal control effort of the MPC, moves the system further away from the reference. In this case, the closed-loop system is called unstable. In other words, the MPC is not ‘strong’ enough to attenuate the impact of the disturbances. This notion of strongness is a property that is commonly called contractivity. In other words, the difference between the actual and the reference trajectory should contract with a minimum rate. How close the actual system comes to the reference depends on the contraction rate, usually denoted by
\rho
, and the worst-case impact of the disturbances, usually denoted by
{\bar{w}}
. These are the quantities you can also find in RMPC formulation (
42
).

A common approach to improve the disturbance attenuation capability of the MPC and ensure that the closed-loop system stays close to the reference trajectory is to design a feedback law executed at a higher frequency than the sampling frequency of the MPC. This way, the impact of disturbances can be diminished before the MPC can do so at the discrete sampling moments. Similar to the design of the terminal control law (
12
) that renders terminal set (
14
) invariant, one could design the feedback law to render a so-called tube around the reference trajectory invariant to the disturbances. This forms the basis for a popular class of RMPC methods: tube MPC
[
37
,
38
,
18
,
39
]
.

Tube MPC is designed to tighten all the constraints by a rigid tube size such that the closed-loop system satisfies the actual constraints if the nominal predicted trajectory satisfies the tightened constraints. Specifically, in tube MPC, the complete predicted trajectory is optimized, including initial state constraint (
42b
). This increases computational complexity but gives a trivial expression for the terminal set
[
40
]
.

Central to the design of tube MPC schemes is to find a suitable expression for the tube. A common approach is to design a feedback law
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
that renders a sublevel set of incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
invariant to the disturbances acting on the system actuated by closed-loop control law (
43
). Such combination of feedback law in
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
and
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
exists if the system satisfies the following assumption
[
30
,
41
]
:

Assumption 3

(Incremental stabilizability)
.

There exist a feedback law
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}:{{{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}}
, an incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}:{{{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}}
that is continuously differentiable and satisfies
{V^{\delta}}({{\bm{z}},{\bm{z}}})=0,\forall{\bm{z}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, parameters
{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}},{c^{\delta\mathrm{,u}}},\rho>0
, and Lipschitz constants
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}>0,j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
and
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}>0
, such that the following properties hold for all
({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})\in{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}}
:

\displaystyle{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}}\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}\rVert^{2}\leq{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}\leq{c^{\delta\mathrm{,u}}}\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}\rVert^{2},

(44a)

\displaystyle{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{x}},{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})})-{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})\leq{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}},\

\displaystyle j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

(44b)

\displaystyle{g^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{x}})-{g^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{z}})\leq{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}},

(44c)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}\leq-2\rho{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})},

(44d)

with
{\dot{\bm{x}}}=f({\bm{x}},{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
and
{\dot{\bm{z}}}=f({{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
. Furthermore, the following norm-like inequality holds
\forall{\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2},{\bm{x}}_{3}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
:

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{3})}\leq\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2})}+\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{2},{\bm{x}}_{3})}.

(45)

In other words, we want to find a squared incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
that quantifies the difference between disturbed trajectory
{\bm{x}}
and nominal, or reference, trajectory
{\bm{z}}
.
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
is lower- and upper-bounded by (
44a
), that contracts at least with rate
2\rho
according to (
44d
), and satisfies norm-like inequality (
45
), such that the system and obstacle avoidance constraints are Lipschitz-bounded by a constant multiplied with the
{\operatorname{sqrt}}
of
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
. The meaning and purpose of the above equations will become more clear later in this section.

Remark 7
.

The superscript
\delta
denotes the incremental property of the Lyapunov function, i.e., the fact that the Lyapunov function describes the difference between two time-varying trajectories
x
and
z
that are close to each other.

Remark 8
.

When comparing the expressions in Assumption
3
with
[
31
, Ass. 1]
, note that
{V^{\delta}}
contains a different number of arguments. In general, one can construct
{V^{\delta}}
that also depends on the nominal input
{\bm{v}}
. However, here, we do not leverage this property and leave the additional argument out for notational convenience.

Thus, if we can upper bound
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}
, we know that we can tighten the system and obstacle avoidance constraints using (
44b
) and (
44c
) to ensure that the open-loop execution of control law (
43
) guarantees that the real system trajectory
{\bm{x}}
satisfies the non-tightened constraints.

Note that applying the
{\operatorname{sqrt}}
operator to (
44d
) gives the following upper bound on the derivative of
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}
:

\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}\leq-\rho\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}.

(46)

This upper bound evolves for
t,\tau\geq 0
as

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+\tau}},{\bm{z}}_{{t+\tau}})}\leq e^{-\rho\tau}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{z}}_{t})}.

(47)

In other words, if the trajectories
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{z}}
deviate from each other, they will contract over time according to (
47
). Correspondingly, the error

{\bm{\delta}}\coloneqq{\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}

(48)

exponentially converges to zero. As mentioned above, this is not the case if disturbances act on the system. Therefore, we would like to find an expression for (
46
) that involves the impact of disturbances
{\bm{w}}
. To this end, let’s first define the following property that holds with the definition of disturbance set (
40
):

Property 2
.

There exists a constant
{\bar{w}}>0
such that for any
({\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}})\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}
and any
{\bm{w}}\in{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}\oplus{\mathcal{W}}
, it holds that

\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}\leq-\rho\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}+{\bar{w}},

(49)

with
{\dot{\bm{x}}}=f({\bm{x}},{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})})+E{\bm{w}}
and
{\dot{\bm{z}}}=f({{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
.

Consequently, if we denote the upper bound of
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}
, also called the tube size, by
s
, i.e.,

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}\leq s,

(50)

we can write the time-derivative of
s
over time as

{\dot{s}}=-\rho s+{\bar{w}}.

(51)

Rewriting (
51
) as a function of time gives

s_{t}=\left(1-e^{-\rho t}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho},

(52)

and taking the limit
t\rightarrow\infty
results in a maximum tube size of

{\bar{s}}=\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}.

(53)

Thus,
s
does not grow unbounded, and the resulting tube size (
53
) is suitable for tightening the constraints in a tube MPC formulation.

In this work, we do not leverage tube MPC, but a slightly different variant: constraint tightening MPC
[
42
,
43
,
15
,
44
]
. This is the formulation used in (
42
). The idea behind constraint tightening MPC is that at the current time
t
, in contrast to tube MPC, the initial predicted state
{\bm{z}}_{{0|t}}
equals the measured state
{\bm{x}}_{t}
(
42b
) and the initial tube size is set to zero (
42c
). From there, given a proper design of closed-loop control law (
43
), the predicted uncertainty grows according to (
51
), which is reflected in (
42e
), with contraction rate
\rho
and worst-case disturbance impact
{\bar{w}}
. The growing tube size is used to tighten the system constraints in (
42f
), using system constraints tightening constants
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
, and obstacle avoidance constraints (
42g
), using obstacle avoidance constraints tightening constant
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}
. Since the tube size does not grow unbounded as given in (
53
),
{\bar{s}}
can be used to construct terminal set constraint (
42h
) such that the system stays within the terminal set for all future times given a suitable terminal control law.

Remark 9
.

As will be shown later,
{\bar{s}}
can be used to tighten the constraints of the reference trajectory, similar to the usage of the terminal set scaling
\alpha
for tightening the constraints of the reference trajectory in the TMPC section.

Similar to Section
3.2
, we need an assumption on the terminal ingredients such that the following terminal cost property is satisfied:

Assumption 4

(Terminal ingredients)
.

There exist a terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}:{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}
and terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}):{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\bar{{\mathcal{X}}}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that the following property holds for all
{\bm{z}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
:

{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})\leq-\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})d\tau,

(54)

with sampling time
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}>0
, optimal solution
({\bm{z}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{\cdot|t}}^{*})
at
t
for all
t\geq 0
and its extended version
{\bm{z}}_{{T+\tau}|t}^{*}
using terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, as defined in Section
4.3.1
.

Next to this assumption on terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
and terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
we want to design terminal set
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
such that tightened system and obstacle avoidance constraints (
42f
) and (
42g
) are satisfied in
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
, respectively, and such that terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
ensures robust positive invariance of
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
. The specific formulation of a tight version of
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
is presented in Section
4.3.2
as part of the proof and therefore not included in Assumption
4
.

Figure 2:
Visualization of a 1D reference trajectory
{\bm{x}}_{t+[0,4{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]}^{\mathrm{r}}
, previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{[0,T]|t}^{*}
, appended part of previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
, candidate solution
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{[0,T]|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
, tube
{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}^{*})\leq s
around
{\bm{z}}_{[0,T]|t}^{*}
, tube
{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}},{\tilde{\bm{z}}})\leq s
around
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{[0,T]|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
, and terminal sets
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
around the reference trajectory corresponding to the previously optimal and the candidate solution. Again, we set
T=4{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
. Compared to Figure
1
,
{\bm{w}}
impacts the system during interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{1}⃝. As a result,
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{0|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\neq{\bm{z}}_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}|t}^{*}
. Note that the tubes and terminal sets are visualized as ellipses to clarify their shapes, whereas they should actually be visualized as vertical lines in this case.

The assumptions above allow us to prove recursive feasibility and trajectory tracking. To provide intuition for these proofs beforehand, similar to Section
3.2
, Figure
2
shows the optimal solution at time
t
and the corresponding candidate solution for time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. Note that, in contrast to the TMPC case, we cannot just take the tail part of the previously optimal solution as a candidate and append it for prediction interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝ since the system is subject to disturbances
{\bm{w}}
during interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{1}⃝. Instead, we measure the disturbed state at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
and leverage the suitably designed feedback term in the control law to steer the candidate back to the previously optimal trajectory. Since the tube size, or equivalently, the constraints tightening in (
42f
) and (
42g
), increases over the horizon, the constraints in interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝ of the candidate are tightened less than the constraints in the same interval of the previously optimal solution. The visual interpretation is that the blue tube is always contained in the orange tube. As a result, it can be shown that the impact of the disturbance during interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{1}⃝ sufficiently contracts by the feedback term in the candidate control law such that the candidate also satisfies the tightened constraints in interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝. The same holds for a suitably designed terminal set. Furthermore, if we append the candidate with terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
in interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝, where the nominal term equals
{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}
and the feedback term steers the candidate towards
{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}
, it can be shown that a suitably designed terminal set is invariant to this control law. Therefore, the candidate also satisfies (
42h
). If the system and obstacle constraints are also satisfied in the terminal set, the satisfaction of (
42f
) and (
42g
) in interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝ trivially follows from the invariance property of the terminal set. Thus, a feasible solution at
t
implies that a feasible solution at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
can be constructed. Therefore, the scheme is recursively feasible.

The trajectory tracking proof entails showing that cost (
42a
) is lower for the candidate than for the previously optimal solution such that convergence to the reference trajectory can be concluded. In the case of TMPC, we could show that cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝ is lower than terminal cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{3}⃝ such that the candidate cost decreases at least by cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{1}⃝ since cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝ is the same. However, cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝ is not the same. In fact, cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{2}⃝ and cost \raisebox{-0.9pt}{4}⃝ both increase depending on the worst-case disturbance impact during interval \raisebox{-0.9pt}{1}⃝. Therefore, strict convergence to the reference trajectory cannot be concluded. Instead, we can prove that the average tracking error gets small.

In summary, showing that RMPC formulation (
42
) ensures safe and efficient mobile robot deployment in the presence of disturbances requires the robot system to be incrementally stabilizable according to Assumption
3
. This means that a feedback controller can be constructed to guarantee that the tracking error of the closed-loop system with respect to the reference trajectory can be quantified and upper-bounded. Furthermore, if the terminal ingredients are suitably designed satisfying Assumption
4
and the candidate is designed as described in Section
4.3.1
, we can prove recursive feasibility in Section
4.3.2
and trajectory tracking with, on average, an error that gets small in Section
4.3.3
. The details of the incremental stabilizability and terminal ingredients design is provided in Section
4.3.4
.

4.3.1
Candidate solution

Similar to Section
3.2.1
, the goal of defining the candidate solution is to show that there exists a feasible, not necessarily optimal, solution to (
42
) at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
given that (
42
) is successfully solved at time
t
. Again, this candidate solution is used to prove recursive feasibility and trajectory tracking and is not necessarily implemented in practice.

In the robust case, we pick a candidate solution similar to the nominal case. For convenience, we define for
\tau\in[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
:

{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}={\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}}),

(55)

with
{\dot{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}
given by (
1
) by applying (
55
).

Given these definitions, we can write the candidate for prediction interval
\tau\in[0,T]
at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
as

\displaystyle{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{0|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}

\displaystyle={\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},

(56a)

\displaystyle{\tilde{s}}_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(56b)

\displaystyle{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\kappa({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}),

(56c)

with
{\dot{\tilde{\bm{z}}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}
and
{\dot{\tilde{s}}}_{\tau}
according to (
42d
) and (
42e
), respectively.

Note that this candidate deviates from the one in Section
3.2.1
. The main difference is that we cannot take the previously optimal state-input trajectory since the state at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
is disturbed compared to prediction
\tau={T^{\mathrm{s}}}
at
t
. Therefore, we need to measure the state at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
and use this as the initial state of the candidate solution. Consequently, following the previously optimal input trajectory
{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},\tau\in[0,T]
