from a different state results in a different trajectory. Thus, this solution cannot be directly used. Instead, we add the feedback law
{\kappa^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}),\tau\in[0,T]
described in Assumption
3
.

4.3.2
Recursive feasibility proof

The recursive feasibility proof follows similar arguments as the proof in Section
3.2.2
with some additional technicalities to account for the disturbances. As mentioned, the first difference is in the satisfaction of (
42b
). Whereas in Section
3.2.2
, we could just take the previously optimal solution, in this case, the state at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
does not equal the nominal predicted one at predicted time
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
at time
t
because of the disturbance effect
{\bm{w}}_{\tau}\neq\bm{0},\tau\in[t,{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}]
. Therefore, we satisfy this constraint by measuring the disturbed state at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
as given by (
56a
) of the candidate.

The initial state constraint, initial tube constraint, system dynamics constraint, and tube dynamics constraint are all trivially satisfied by (
56
). That leaves us to show that the (tightened) system constraints (
42f
), the (tightened) obstacle avoidance constraints (
42g
), and the terminal set constraint (
42h
) are satisfied in the interval
\tau\in[0,T]
.

Similar to the procedure in Section
3.2.2
, we split the interval into two subintervals,
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
and
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
, to show satisfaction of (
42f
) and (
42g
).

System constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})}

Let’s start with the proofs for the interval
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
. For this interval, (
42f
) are satisfied by candidate (
56
) if the following holds for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}\leq 0.

(57)

We know that for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
the previously optimal solution satisfies

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}\leq 0.

(58)

Therefore, we would like to create an upper bound on
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})
in terms of
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})
. This can be done using the Lipschitz assumption (
44b
) under control law
\kappa({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})
. This gives the following upper bound for
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})\leq{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}.

(59)

Filling in (
59
) in the left-hand side of (
57
) gives for
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}\leq{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}.

(60)

Thus, a sufficient condition to satisfy (
57
) for
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
is the following:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}\leq 0.

(61)

We know that (
58
) is satisfied by solving (
42
) at
t
. Therefore, to show that (
61
) holds, we need to show that the following inequality holds for
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
:

{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}\leq{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau},

(62)

Dividing the left-hand and right-hand sides by
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}>0,j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
gives

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}+s_{\tau}\leq s_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}.

(63)

We know that
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
is upper-bounded by tube size
s
according to (
50
) and that the evolution of
s
over time is given by (
52
). Thus, after
{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
, the tube has the following size:

s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}=\left(1-e^{\rho{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho},

(64)

and we can write the following upper bound on
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{0|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}|t}}^{*})}
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
:

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{0|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}|t}}^{*})}\leq s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}.

(65)

By applying control law
\kappa({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})
, to the system, we can show that the candidate response
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}
will contract to the previously optimal nominal prediction
{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}
with factor
\rho
over time according to (
47
). Thus, by iteratively applying the contractivity, we can write the following upper bound for
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}
:

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}\leq e^{-\rho\tau}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}.

(66)

Filling in (
66
) in (
63
) yields the following sufficient condition:

e^{-\rho\tau}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+s_{\tau}\leq s_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau},

(67)

or, alternatively,

s_{\tau}\leq s_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}-e^{-\rho\tau}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}.

(68)

Appendix
B
shows that (
68
) holds for
\tau\geq 0
, which includes
\tau\in[0,T]
. Therefore, the system constraints are satisfied for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
and thus for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
. Note that we cannot prove satisfaction for
\tau\in[0,T]
since this reasoning relies on the fact that (
58
) holds, which is only enforced for
\tau\in[0,T]
at
t
, and thus for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
.

Obstacle avoidance constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})}

For interval
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
, we know that (
42g
) are satisfied by candidate (
56
) if the following holds for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}s_{\tau}\leq 0.

(69)

We know that for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
the previously optimal solution satisfies

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}s_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}\leq 0.

(70)

Therefore, similar to the system constraints proof for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
, we would like to create an upper bound on
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})
in terms of
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})
. Lipschitz assumption (
44c
) gives the following upper bound for
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}}
:

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}})\leq{g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}.

(71)

By following the same steps as for the system constraints, we can show that the proof boils down to showing that (
68
) holds, which is true by the combination of the proof in Appendix
B
and Assumption
2
from Section
3.2.2
. Thus, the obstacle avoidance constraints are also satisfied for
\tau\in[0,T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
.

Terminal set constraint satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]}

Next, we prove that if (
42h
) is satisfied at time
t
, candidate (
56
) satisfies (
42h
) in prediction interval
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. Before proving this, we need to know a suitable expression for the terminal set. In the TMPC case,
{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
was chosen as a sublevel set of
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
. If we use this set in the robust case, it means that candidate (
56
) should satisfy this constraint for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
. As discussed, the minimum terminal set scaling
\alpha
could be chosen equal to 0 in the case of TMPC. However, even if the previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{[0,T]|t}^{*}
would exactly overlap with reference
{\bm{x}}_{t+[0,T]}^{\mathrm{r}}
, the disturbance impact during interval
[t,{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}]
renders terminal set constraint (
42h
) infeasible for candidate (
56
) since we only have a finite horizon and exponential contraction of the disturbance impact as given by (
66
). In other words, candidate
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
deviates from previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
. Therefore, we need another expression for the minimum terminal set that accounts for the disturbance impact during
[t,{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}]
.

Besides the mentioned disturbance impact, we also need to account for the fact that it is likely to happen that the previously optimal solution does not overlap with the reference for
\tau\in[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, i.e.,
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}\neq{\bm{x}}_{t+[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]}^{\mathrm{r}}
, on a disturbed system, even if such a system has exponential disturbance rejection properties by closed-loop terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
.

In summary, to show that candidate (
56
) satisfies terminal set constraint (
42h
) for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at time
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
, we need a terminal set that accounts for both the error between previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
and reference
{\bm{x}}_{t+[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]}^{\mathrm{r}}
and the error between candidate
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
and previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
.

The error between
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
and
{\bm{x}}_{t+[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]}^{\mathrm{r}}
contracts with rate
\rho
according to (
47
). For
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
it is given by

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}}_{T+\tau|t}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{t+T+\tau}^{\mathrm{r}})}\leq e^{-\rho\tau}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{T|t}}^{\mathrm{r}})}.

(72)

The error between
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
and
{\bm{z}}_{[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]|t}^{*}
is caused by the described disturbance impact during
[t,{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}]
and also contracts with rate
\rho
according to the following expression for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
:

\displaystyle\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{T+\tau|t}^{*})}\overset{\eqref{eq:vd_bound_s_tau}}{\leq}

\displaystyle e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}.

(73)

We can construct an upper bound on the total tracking error of the candidate using (
45
) in Assumption
3
. For
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
this yields

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{t+T+\tau}^{\mathrm{r}})}\leq\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}}_{T+\tau|t}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{t+T+\tau}^{\mathrm{r}})}+\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{T+\tau|t}^{*})}.

(74)

Therefore, we can write the upper bound on the total terminal tracking error for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
as

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{t+T+\tau}^{\mathrm{r}})}\leq e^{-\rho\tau}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})}+e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}.

(75)

To prove that the candidate satisfies the terminal set constraint given this inequality, we leverage the expression of the terminal set proposed in
[
41
]
:

{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{z}}\in{\mathcal{X}},s_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \middle|\ \sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})}+s_{T}\leq\alpha\right\},

(76)

with
s_{T}
representing the tube size evaluated at
T
according to (
52
) and
\alpha
denoting the terminal set scaling, similar to the definition of
\alpha
in (
14
). Note that the terminal set is expressed in non-squared form, so we use notation
\alpha
instead of
\alpha^{2}
.

Extending this terminal set to prediction interval
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
gives

{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+T+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}})\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{z}}_{{T+\tau}|t}\in{\mathcal{X}},s_{{T+\tau}}\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \middle|\ \sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}}_{{T+\tau}|t},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}})}+s_{{T+\tau}}\leq\alpha\right\}.

(77)

We know that pair
({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}}^{*},s_{T})
satisfies terminal set constraint (
76
). Therefore, it holds that

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})}\leq\alpha-s_{T},

(78)

which leads to the following expression for (
75
) for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
:

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{t+T+\tau}^{\mathrm{r}})}\leq e^{-\rho\tau}(\alpha-s_{T})+e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}.

(79)

We can use this expression to derive a minimum value for terminal set scaling
\alpha
such that
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
satisfies the terminal set constraint for
\tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
, i.e.,

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{t+T+\tau}^{\mathrm{r}})}\leq\alpha-s_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}.

(80)

This inequality is satisfied if the addition of the two errors described above, i.e., the right-hand side of (
79
), is upper-bounded by the maximum error allowed in the terminal set, i.e., the right-hand side of (
80
):

e^{-\rho\tau}(\alpha-s_{T})+e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}\leq\alpha-s_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau},

(81)

which trivially holds for
\tau=0
by Appendix
B
. Working out this inequality for
\tau\in(0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
gives the following lower bound on
\alpha
:

\displaystyle e^{-\rho\tau}(\alpha-s_{T})+e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\alpha-s_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}

(82a)

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\alpha+e^{-\rho\tau}s_{T}-s_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}s_{T^{\mathrm{s}}}

(82b)

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\alpha+e^{-\rho\tau}\left(1-e^{-\rho T}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}-\left(1-e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(c)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}\left(1-e^{-\rho{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

(82c)

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\alpha+\left(e^{-\rho\tau}-e^{-\rho({T+\tau})}-1+e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(d)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle\left(e^{-\rho(T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau)}-e^{-\rho({T+\tau})}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

(82d)

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\alpha+\left(e^{-\rho\tau}-1\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(e)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle 0

(82e)

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\alpha-\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(f)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle 0

(82f)

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\alpha

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(g)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle\left(1-e^{-\rho\tau}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}

(82g)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(h)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho},

(82h)

where (a) is obtained by setting the upper bound of (
79
) smaller equal to (
80
), (b) by re-ordering terms and combining
\alpha
multiplication factors, (c) by writing out the tube dynamics according to (
52
), (d) by combining
\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
multiplication factors, (e) by canceling common terms on both sides and (f)-(h) by trivially working out the inequality.

In other words,
\alpha
should be at least as large as the maximum tube size given in (
53
). This makes sense since this means that terminal control law
{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}
can keep the system within that tube around the reference trajectory, guaranteeing that applying this solution will keep the system safe at all times. Note that
\alpha
can also be chosen larger than
\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
, similar to the discussion in Section
3.2.4
. This will result in a larger terminal set, which will render (
42
) easier to solve, but it will also increase conservatism in planning the reference trajectory.

Remark 10
.

An alternative to the proof above is to show terminal set constraint satisfaction for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
by first proving that candidate
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}
and corresponding tube
s_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
satisfy terminal set constraint (
76
) and then leveraging the fact that the terminal set is invariant for
\alpha\geq\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
, as shown in Appendix
C
.

System constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]}

As mentioned before, given that tube inequality (
68
) is proven to hold for
\tau\in[0,T]
, the only thing we need to ensure satisfaction of candidate system constraints (
57
) for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
is to show that system constraints of previously optimal solution (
58
) are satisfied for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
at
{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}
, or, equivalently, for
\tau\in[T,T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}]
at
t
, given that terminal set (
76
) is invariant.

Appendix
C
proves that terminal set (
76
) is invariant for
({\bm{z}}_{[T,{T+\tau}]|t}^{*},s_{{T+\tau}}),\tau\geq 0
under the same condition
\alpha\geq\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
as given in (
82
). Given the proof of (
68
) from before, candidate system constraints (
57
) are satisfied for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
under the following assumption:

Assumption 5

(System constraints tightening for reference trajectory)
.

The reference trajectory satisfies the tightened systems constraints, i.e., (
7
) holds, with
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})
given by

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})={g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{u}}})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\alpha,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

(83)

where
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}
is defined in (
44b
) for
j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
and
\alpha\geq\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
.

This assumption is similar to the design of (
33
) in Section
3.2.4
, but with the required lower bound
\alpha\geq\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
to account for the effect of disturbances.

Obstacle avoidance constraints satisfaction for
\bm{\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]}

Proving that candidate obstacle avoidance constraints (
69
) are satisfied for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
follows the same arguments as the proof for the system constraints for
\tau\in[T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}},T]
under the combination of Assumption
2
and a similar assumption as above:

Assumption 6

(Obstacle avoidance constraints tightening for reference trajectory)
.

The reference trajectory satisfies the tightened obstacle avoidance constraints, i.e., (
9
) holds, with
{g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,o}}}({\bm{p}})
given by

{g_{j}^{\mathrm{r,o}}}({\bm{p}})={g_{j}^{\mathrm{o}}}({\bm{p}})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\alpha,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}},

(84)

where
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}
is defined in (
44c
) and
\alpha\geq\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
.

This assumption is similar to the design of (
35
) in Section
3.2.4
but with the required lower bound
\alpha\geq\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
to account for the effect of disturbances.
∎

4.3.3
Trajectory tracking proof

The trajectory tracking proof in this section follows similar arguments as the one presented in Section
3.2.3
. For convenience, let’s define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}}),

(85)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|t}}^{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}),

(86)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t}),

(87)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}),

(88)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}),

(89)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}),

(90)

and start by following the same proof as in (
22
) in Section
3.2.3
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}},

(91a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}.

(91b)

So far, the proof for TMPC and RMPC is the same. However, note that the stage costs are not equal at the same points in time, i.e.,
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})\neq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}),\tau\in[0,T]
, since the candidate
{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}
deviates from the previously optimal solution
{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}
. Therefore, (c) in (
22
) does not hold. Specifically, for
\tau\in[0,T]
, candidate stage cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})
is defined as

{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})=\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2},

(92)

and previously optimal stage cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})
as

{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})=\lVert{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}.

(93)

Writing out the difference between the two gives

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})

\displaystyle=\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}-\lVert{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}-\lVert{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}-\lVert{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}.

(94)

Note that this expression is continuously differentiable in compact set
{\mathcal{Z}}\times{\bar{\mathcal{Z}}}
. Therefore, it is Lipschitz continuous in this set, and the following inequality holds for
\tau\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}-\lVert{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}-\lVert{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{u}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},x}}}\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert+{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},u}}}\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert.

(95)

This result shows that we can find an upper bound on the difference in stage costs.

Ideally, we would like to find the smallest possible value for this upper bound. We can rewrite (
44a
) in Assumption
3
as

\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}\rVert\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}.

(96)

Inserting this upper bound in (
4.3.3
) gives for
\tau\in[0,T]
:

{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},x}}}\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert\leq\frac{{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},x}}}}{\sqrt{{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}.

(97)

In other words, the difference between candidate and previously optimal states is upper-bounded by some other Lipschitz constant
\frac{{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},x}}}}{\sqrt{{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}}}}
multiplied with the incremental Lyapunov function evaluated at the corresponding point.

To refine the expression for
{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},u}}}\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert
, note that (
44b
) implies that control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}
is Lipschitz continuous given that the inputs are constrained by a box, as defined in (
2
). Thus, the following holds for
\tau\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\lVert{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}

\displaystyle\lVert\kappa({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})-{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{=}

\displaystyle\lVert{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}+{\kappa^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})-{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(c)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\bar{\kappa}^{\delta}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})},

(98)

where (a) is obtained by filling in (
56c
), (b) by filling in control law definition (
43
), and (c) by the resulting Lipschitz bound on the feedback law in
{\kappa^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})
with constant
{\bar{\kappa}^{\delta}}
.

Combining (
4.3.3
) and (
4.3.3
) and filling in upper bounds (
97
) and (
4.3.3
) gives the following stage cost upper bound for
\tau\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},x}}}}{\sqrt{{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}}}}+{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},u}}}{\bar{\kappa}^{\delta}}\right)\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},xu}}}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}.

(99)

Using the upper bound for
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*})}
in (
66
), we can define the following upper bound on the stage costs for
\tau\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})

\displaystyle\leq{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}},xu}}}e^{-\rho\tau}\left(1-e^{\rho{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}\eqqcolon\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),

(100)

or, alternatively,

{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\tilde{\bm{v}}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})\leq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}|t}}^{*},{\bm{r}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+\tau}})+\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}).

(101)

In other words, this means that the candidate stage cost is maximum
\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
away from the previously optimal stage cost at prediction time
\tau\in[0,T]
.

Next to the stage costs, the terminal costs of the candidate and extended previously optimal solution (
56
) are not equal, i.e.,
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})\neq{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})
. Since the terminal cost is also continuously differentiable in compact set
{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\bar{{\mathcal{X}}}}}
, it is Lipschitz continuous. Therefore, we can write a similar inequality as for the difference in stage costs (
4.3.3
):

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle=\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{P}^{2}-\lVert{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{P}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}},x}}}\lVert{\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}-{\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*}\rVert.

(102)

Following the same steps above, we can write the upper bound on the difference in terminal costs as

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{*},{\bm{x}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})

\displaystyle\leq{L^{\mathrm{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}}}}e^{-\rho T}\left(1-e^{\rho{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}\eqqcolon\alpha^{\mathrm{f}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}).

(103)

We can now further work out the trajectory tracking proof in (
91
) by replacing the equality for (c) in (
22
) with the inequality in (
101
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}

(104a)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}

(104b)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(c)}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}

(104c)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|t}}^{\mathrm{f}}-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}

(104d)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(e)}}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{f}}-{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|t}}^{\mathrm{f}}+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

(104e)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(f)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{J}}_{{{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}+T}|t}}^{\mathrm{f}}+\alpha^{\mathrm{f}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})-{\mathcal{J}}_{{T|t}}^{\mathrm{f}}+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau

(104f)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(g)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+\alpha^{\mathrm{f}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),

(104g)

where (a) is obtained by (
91
), (b) by filling in (
101
) in
\int_{0}^{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau
and
\int_{T-{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau
, (c), (d), and (e) by following the same steps as (d), (e), and (f) in (
22
), (f) by (
4.3.3
), and (g) by (
54
) in Assumption
4
. Leveraging (
54
) in Assumption
4
gives

{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}\leq-\int_{0}^{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\mathcal{J}}_{{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{s}}d\tau+\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+\alpha^{\mathrm{f}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}).

(105)

Thus, we get the same descent bound for
{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}
as in Section
3.2.3
, up to

{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})\coloneqq\int_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{T}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+\int_{T}^{T+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\alpha_{\tau}^{\mathrm{s}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})d\tau+\alpha^{\mathrm{f}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}).

(106)

Similar to Section
3.2.3
, we prove convergence by first noting that the descent property of the optimal cost (
105
) implies

{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}\leq-c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau+{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),

(107)

with constant
c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}>0
. Re-arranging the terms gives

c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau\leq{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}+{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}).

(108)

Instead of
\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}
we are interested in the actual tracking error
\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}
, which is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{\leq}c^{\mathrm{t}}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}\rVert+\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}

(109a)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{\leq}c^{\mathrm{t,1}}s_{\tau}+\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}

(109b)

where (a) is obtained by a Lipschitz bound with constant
c^{\mathrm{t}}
and (b) by another Lipschitz bound with constant
c^{\mathrm{t,1}}
on the norm using a combination of (
44a
), (
50
), and (
52
). Therefore, we can write a similar upper bound as in (
108
) but now for
\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}
:

\displaystyle c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leq c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+c^{\mathrm{t,1}}s_{\tau}d\tau

(110a)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}\lVert{\bm{z}}_{\tau-t|t}^{*}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau+c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{t}^{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}c^{\mathrm{t,1}}s_{\tau}d\tau

(110b)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{\leq}{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{{t+{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}}^{*}+{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),

(110c)

with
{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})\coloneqq{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})+c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}c^{\mathrm{t,1}}\left({T^{\mathrm{s}}}-\frac{1}{\rho}\left(1-e^{-\rho{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\right)\right)\frac{{\bar{w}}}{\rho}
, and where (a) is obtained by writing out the integral expression and (b) by (
108
). Iterating this inequality from
0
to
t=2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}
yields

\displaystyle c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}\int_{0}^{2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau\leq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{0}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{*}+{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})+{\mathcal{J}}_{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{*}+{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})

(111a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{0}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{*}+2{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})

(111b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{0}^{*}-{\mathcal{J}}_{2{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{*}+\frac{t}{{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}),

(111c)

In contrast to (
25
) in Section
3.2.3
, iterating this inequality for
t\rightarrow\infty
is not necessarily bounded for the worst-case disturbance impact. Therefore, we can conclude that the average of this inequality gets small by dividing it by
t
and leveraging the fact that
{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}
is uniformly bounded:

\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{{\operatorname{lim}}}\frac{\int_{0}^{t}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau}{t}\leq\frac{{\mathcal{J}}_{0}^{*}-\underset{t\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{lim}}{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}}{c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}t}+\frac{1}{c^{{\mathcal{J}},\mathrm{d}}{T^{\mathrm{s}}}}{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}}).

(112)

Thus, by applying Barbalat’s lemma
[
29
]
on
\frac{\int_{0}^{t}\lVert{\bm{x}}_{\tau}-{\bm{x}}_{\tau}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}d\tau}{t}
we conclude that, on average, the tracking error
\lVert{\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\mathrm{r}}\rVert
gets small over time, proportional to
{\bar{\alpha}^{\mathrm{s,f,1}}}(\rho,{\bar{w}},{T^{\mathrm{s}}})
. Note that this conclusion makes sense in the presence of unpredictable and bounded disturbances. ∎

4.3.4
Design to satisfy the assumptions

This section will detail the design of:

•

the obstacle avoidance constraints, which need to satisfy Assumption
2
;

•

incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
and corresponding Lipschitz constants
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
and
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}
, which need to satisfy Assumption
3
;

•

the terminal ingredients, which need to satisfy Assumption
4
;

•

the reference trajectory, which needs to satisfy Assumptions
5
and
6
.

Obstacle avoidance constraints design

To prove recursive feasibility, we need the obstacle avoidance constraint to satisfy Assumption
2
. We can achieve this by following the same design as written in Section
3.2.4
.

Incremental stabilizability design

The incremental stabilizability design follows a similar procedure to the terminal ingredients design in Section
3.2.4
. The goal is to compute a suitable incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
that is bounded by tube size
s
given in (
42e
) and corresponding control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}
that satisfy properties (
44a
)-(
45
).

Remark 11
.

The most important difference with the design in Section
3.2.4
is the fact that
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
should satisfy contraction property (
44d
) that is used to prove trajectory tracking and recursive feasibility in the presence of disturbances. Furthermore, control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}
is used in the proofs and implemented in closed-loop execution according to (
43
).

We can write the dynamics of
{\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}}
as follows:

\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}=\frac{d}{dt}\left({V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{2}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}.

(113)

Since it is easier to find an expression for
\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
rather than
\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}
, we rewrite (
113
) as

\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}=2\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}.

(114)

Filling in desired tube dynamics (
42e
) gives the following upper bound on
\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
:

\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}\leq 2\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}\left(-\rho{\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}}+{\bar{w}}\right)=-2\rho{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}+2\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}{\bar{w}}.

(115)

Thus, the goal is to find a suitable incremental Lyapunov function of which the upper bound can be written in the form (
115
).

Using control contraction metrics (CCMs)
[
45
]
, we can define the incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
as

{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}\coloneqq\underset{{\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}\in C({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}{{\operatorname{min}}}\int_{0}^{1}{\frac{\partial{\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}}^{\top}{P^{\delta}({\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)})}{\frac{\partial{\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}}ds,

(116)

with metric
{P^{\delta}({\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)})}
and where
{\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}
is a curve in set of curves

C({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}\ \middle|\ {\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}}(0)={\bm{z}},{\bm{c}^{\mathrm{x}}}(1)={\bm{x}}\right\}.

(117)

{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}\in C({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})
denotes the geodesic: the curve that minimizes
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
.

We know that the evolutions of the nominal and disturbed state are described by (
1
) and (
39
), respectively. Thus, we know the evolution of the endpoints
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}}(0)
and
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}}(1)
of geodesic
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}
over time. A logical candidate for the evolution of
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},s\in[0,1]
, over time, i.e., one that matches
{\dot{\bm{z}}}
and
{\dot{\bm{x}}}
for
s=0
and
s=1
, respectively, is thus given by

{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}\coloneqq f({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})+E({\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}+s{\bm{w}}^{0}),

(118)

with control law
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)}
that is obtained by integrating the feedback law over the geodesic:

{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)}\coloneqq{\bm{v}}+\int_{0}^{s}{K^{\delta}}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}({\tilde{s}})}){\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}({\tilde{s}})}}{\partial{\tilde{s}}}}d{\tilde{s}}.

(119)

Similarly, a candidate for the evolution of the path derivative of the geodesic over time is given by

\frac{d}{dt}{\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}}\coloneqq{{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})}{\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}}+E{\bm{w}}^{0},

(120)

with closed-loop dynamics

{{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})}\coloneqq{A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})}{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)})},

(121)

and Jacobians

{A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})}\coloneqq\frac{\partial{f^{\mathrm{w}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{w}})}}{\partial{\bm{x}}}\left.\right|_{({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})},\quad{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})}\coloneqq\frac{\partial{f^{\mathrm{w}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{w}})}}{\partial{\bm{u}}}\left.\right|_{({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)})},

(122)

since, locally, the nonlinear dynamics are given by its Jacobians.

Using definitions (
118
) and (
120
) for
{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}
and
\frac{d}{dt}{\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}}
, respectively, and temporarily using the following shorthand notations:

\displaystyle{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)},

(123a)

\displaystyle{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)},

(123b)

\displaystyle{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})},

(123c)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\coloneqq{A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})},

(123d)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\coloneqq{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})},

(123e)

\displaystyle{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}},

(123f)

\displaystyle{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{d}{dt}{\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}},

(123g)

\displaystyle{P^{\delta}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})},

(123h)

we can write the time evolution of the upper bound of
\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
in the form (
115
) using candidates
{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}
and
{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}+{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{\dot{P}^{\delta}}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}+{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}ds

(124a)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}\left({A^{\mathrm{cl}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}+{\dot{P}^{\delta}}+{P^{\delta}}{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}\right){{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}+\left(E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right)^{\top}{P^{\delta}}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}+{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}E{\bm{w}}^{0}ds

(124b)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}\left({A^{\mathrm{cl}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}+{\dot{P}^{\delta}}+{P^{\delta}}g{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}\right){{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}+2{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}E{\bm{w}}^{0}ds

(124c)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}\left({A^{\mathrm{cl}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}+{\dot{P}^{\delta}}+{P^{\delta}}{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}\right){{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}ds+2\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}E{\bm{w}}^{0}ds

(124d)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}\left({A^{\mathrm{cl}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}+{\dot{P}^{\delta}}+{P^{\delta}}{A^{\mathrm{cl}}}\right){{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}ds+2\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{P^{\delta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}E{\bm{w}}^{0}ds

(124e)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{\leq}-2\rho\int_{0}^{1}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}}}ds+2\int_{0}^{1}\left\lVert{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}{P^{\delta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rVert\left\lVert{P^{\delta}}^{\frac{1}{2}}E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right\rVert ds

(124f)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{=}-2\rho{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}+2\int_{0}^{1}\left\lVert{{\bm{\gamma}}_{s}^{\mathrm{x}^{\top}}}\right\rVert_{{P^{\delta}}}\left\lVert E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right\rVert_{{P^{\delta}}}ds

(124g)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(c)}}{\leq}-2\rho{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}+2\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}{\bar{w}},

(124h)

for
{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}
where (a) is obtained by the combination of the following contraction LMI
[
45
]
:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\dot{P}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\left({A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\right)+

(125)

\displaystyle\left({A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\right)^{\top}{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+2\rho{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\preceq 0,

with
{\dot{P}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\coloneqq\frac{\partial{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}}{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}}}{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\mathrm{x}}}
and contraction rate
\rho>0
, which is based on the contraction analysis for nonlinear systems as presented in
[
46
]
, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (b) by the definition of
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
in (
116
), and (c) by the following definition of
{\bar{w}}
:

{\bar{w}}\coloneqq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}\\
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}}\in C({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})\end{subarray}}{max}\left\lVert E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right\rVert_{\bar{P}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})},

(126)

with

{\bar{P}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\coloneqq{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}}\left({P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\right){I^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}},

(127)

where
{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}}({P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})})
denotes the maximum eigenvalue of matrix
{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
.

Remark 12
.

Since we use shorthand notations (
123a
) and (
123b
), we can evaluate LMI (
125
) for an infinitely large number of points
s\in[0,1]
on the geodesic. Thus, (
125
) can also be considered a set of LMIs. Since the constraints tightening is such that
({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})\in{\mathcal{Z}},s\in[0,1]

[
19
, Prop. 5]
, it suffices to grid
{\mathcal{Z}}
in a similar way as described in Section
3.2.4
.

Remark 13
.

Compared to
[
45
]
, (
125
) with shorthand notation (
123
), does not contain the time-dependency since we do not consider a time-varying system.

Note that (
126
) confirms that Property
2
holds for compact disturbance set (
40
).

Note also that (
125
) contains the multiplication of
{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
and
{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
. Therefore, it is a bilinear LMI in the context of an SDP in which both
{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
and
{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
are decision variables. Under change of coordinates
{X^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}={P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}^{-1}
and
{Y^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}={K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}^{-1}
(
125
) translates to the following convex contraction LMI
[
45
]
:

\displaystyle-{\dot{X}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+{A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{X^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{Y^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+

(128)

\displaystyle\left({A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{X^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{Y^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\right)^{\top}+2\rho{X^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\preceq 0.

Thus, any combination of
{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
and corresponding control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}
that is obtained by integrating over the geodesic, i.e.,

{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}={{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}}(1),

(129)

with
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}(s)}
given in (
119
), that satisfies LMI (
128
) ensures that the closed-loop trajectory
{\bm{x}}
will exponentially contract towards
{\bm{z}}
with rate
\rho
. For a more elaborate exposition of this result, please refer to
[
19
]
and references therein
[
46
,
45
,
18
,
39
,
47
]
.

In general,
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}
is a nonlinear curve, so evaluating (
116
) as part of terminal set (
76
) in the solver is computationally expensive. To reduce solve time, we choose a state-independent incremental Lyapunov function, i.e.,
{P^{\delta}}
instead of
{P^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
, such that the geodesic becomes a straight line connecting
{\bm{z}}
and
{\bm{x}}
. In this case, the geodesic is mathematically described by

{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}={\bm{z}}+s({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}})

(130)

and

{\frac{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}}{\partial s}}={\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}.

(131)

As a result, contraction LMI (
128
) changes to

{A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{X^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{Y^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+\left({A({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{X^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}+{B({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}},{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}})}{Y^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}\right)^{\top}+2\rho{X^{\delta}}\preceq 0,

(132)

in which the term
-{\dot{X}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
disappears compared to (
128
) as the time derivative is given by
{\dot{X}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}=\frac{\partial{\dot{X}^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}}{\partial{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}}}{\dot{{\bm{\gamma}}}^{\mathrm{x}}}
. Furthermore, incremental Lyapunov function (
116
) simplifies to the following quadratic expression:

{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}=({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}})^{T}{P^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}),

(133)

or, equivalently,

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}=\lVert{\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}\rVert_{{P^{\delta}}},

(134)

and the control law is given by

{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}={{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{u}}}(1)={\bm{v}}+\int_{0}^{1}{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)})}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}})ds.

(135)

Note that this implies that (
44a
) is trivially satisfied with
{c^{\delta\mathrm{,l}}}={\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}}({P^{\delta}})
and
{c^{\delta\mathrm{,u}}}={\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}}({P^{\delta}})
, where
{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}}({P^{\delta}})
and
{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}}({P^{\delta}})
denote the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of
{P^{\delta}}
, respectively, and (
45
) is also satisfied. Correspondingly, we can constant
{\bar{w}}
in (
49
) as

{\bar{w}}\coloneqq\underset{{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\operatorname{vert}}({\mathcal{W}})}{{\operatorname{max}}}\lVert E{\bm{w}}^{0}\rVert_{{P^{\delta}}},

(136)

since
{\mathcal{W}}
is a convex polytopic set. The choice for a state-independent
{P^{\delta}}
increases conservatism since the resulting curve length is larger than nonlinear geodesic
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}
in parts of the state space where Jacobians (
122
) are highly nonlinear. Consequently, integrating the control law over this curve results in a sub-optimal control strategy. This control strategy will result in a contraction rate of at least
\rho
, but it will be slightly less effective than the control strategy based on a nonlinear geodesic.

To reduce conservatism, we leverage a state-dependent feedback gain
{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
.
{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}})}
is computed outside the solver using (
129
), so computation time is less important for enabling real-time implementation.

Remark 14
.

Note that CCM methods provide a way to ensure uniform exponential stability, i.e., exponential stability guarantees that hold in the complete state space, even though metric
{P^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}
is state-dependent. This contrasts with the design of tracking LMI (
27
), which includes reference-dependent matrix
P({\bm{r}})
, but is only valid in local regions
\epsilon
around the reference trajectory. In practice, the value of
\epsilon
for which the results hold is hard to obtain. Therefore, to obtain strict safety guarantees in the robust case, it is practically recommended to use CCMs according to the design above or remove the state dependency in
{P^{\delta}}
. Using CCMs, uniform exponential stabilizability follows from computing integral expressions in the metric space. Using state-independent
{P^{\delta}}
, uniform exponential stabilizability follows as a direct consequence of the mean value theorem (MVT) since choosing a constant CCM
{P^{\delta}}
results in a straight line minimizing geodesic
{{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)}
between
{\bm{z}}
and
{\bm{x}}
as described above.

Contraction LMI (
128
) is the main property that enables the robust recursive feasibility and trajectory tracking proofs to hold. The resulting metric
{P^{\delta}}
is used to construct incremental Lyapunov function (
116
) that satisfies (
44d
). Consequently, tube (
50
) grows to a robust positive invariant (RPI) set:

\sqrt{{V^{\delta}}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{z}}_{t})}\leq{\bar{s}},\ t\geq 0

(137)

with
{\bar{s}}
defined in (
53
). To reduce conservatism we optimize the shape of (
137
) by satisfying the following RPI LMI:

\begin{bmatrix}{A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}+\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{\top}+\lambda{X^{\delta}}&E{\bm{w}}^{0}\\
\left(E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right)^{\top}&-\lambda\delta^{2}\end{bmatrix}\preceq 0,

(138)

with
{\bm{\zeta}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
,
{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\operatorname{vert}}({\mathcal{W}})
and multiplier
\lambda\geq 0
. This LMI enforces that sublevel set
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}\leq\delta^{2}
is RPI for disturbed dynamics (
39
) for all
{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}
and with
\delta\coloneqq{\bar{s}}
. Note that
\rho
is a hyperparameter and
{\bar{w}}
can be computed after optimizing for
{P^{\delta}}
using (
136
). Therefore, without loss of generality, we can set
\delta=1
, which helps to bridge between expressions in quadratic form
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
and linear form
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}
. Furthermore, note that, since the disturbances enter linearly in system dynamics (
39
) and the set
{\mathcal{W}}
is convex, it suffices to evaluate a set of LMIs of the form (
138
) at the vertices of
{\mathcal{W}}
. Appendix
D
provides the proof for (
138
).

Remark 15
.

An alternative to designing the RPI set using polytopic set
{\mathcal{W}}
is to leverage universal
{\mathcal{L}_{\infty}}
-gain bounds on disturbances
{\bm{w}}^{0}
as presented in
[
18
]
.

In contrast to the TMPC design, in which terminal set scaling
\alpha
is computed based on the desired distance to obstacles
{d_{\mathrm{max}}}
, the terminal set and corresponding RPI set of
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
have a minimum size caused by disturbances
{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}
. To prevent the sets from becoming large, thus resulting in conservative behavior,
{P^{\delta}}
and
{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}
are computed using the following SDP, in which the tightening constants
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
and
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}
, normalized with respect to the corresponding constraint interval, are minimized:

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{X^{\delta}},{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\\
{{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{2},{{c^{\mathrm{o}}}}^{2}\end{subarray}}{{\operatorname{min}}}\ \

\displaystyle{c^{\mathrm{c,o}}}{{c^{\mathrm{o}}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{{n^{\mathrm{s}}}}{c_{j}^{\mathrm{c,s}}}{{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{2},

(139a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle{X^{\delta}}\succeq 0,

(139b)

\displaystyle{A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}+\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{\top}+2\rho{X^{\delta}}\preceq 0,

(139c)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}+\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{\top}+\lambda{X^{\delta}}&E{\bm{w}}^{0}\\
\left(E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right)^{\top}&-\lambda\end{bmatrix}\preceq 0,

(139d)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}}^{2}&{L_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\begin{bmatrix}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\\
{X^{\delta}}\end{bmatrix}\\
\left({L_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\begin{bmatrix}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\\
{X^{\delta}}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{\top}&{X^{\delta}}\end{bmatrix}\succeq 0,\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

(139e)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{{c^{\mathrm{o}}}}^{2}I^{{n^{\mathrm{p}}}}&M{X^{\delta}}\\
\left(M{X^{\delta}}\right)^{\top}&{X^{\delta}}\end{bmatrix}\succeq 0,

(139f)

\displaystyle{\bm{\zeta}}\in{\mathcal{Z}},\quad{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\operatorname{vert}}\left({\mathcal{W}}\right),

with contraction rate
\rho>0
and multiplier
\lambda\geq 0
that can be computed using bi-section over this SDP and
\delta=1
.

Note that LMI (
139e
), to ensure Lipschitz continuity of the system constraints, is the same as (
38
). We can derive similar a LMI (
139f
) to ensure Lipschitz continuity of the obstacle avoidance constraints. The proof is similar to the proof for (
38
) as written in
[
32
]
and included in Appendix
E
for completeness.

Similar to SDP (
29
), SDP (
139
) is semi-infinite, so we need to evaluate the expressions at grid points and vertices, both in
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
to account for the effect of disturbances.

Remark 16
.

Note that LMI (
139d
) has to be evaluated at all grid points in both
{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
, meaning that the number of LMIs equals the multiplication of the number of grid points in both sets. This might result in a drastic increase in (offline) computation time. To alleviate this effect, we can introduce decision variable
{\bar{{\mathcal{W}}}}
and use the following combination of LMIs that is sufficient to ensure the satisfaction of (
139d
):

\begin{bmatrix}0^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}&E{\bm{w}}^{0}\\
\left(E{\bm{w}}^{0}\right)^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}\preceq{\bar{{\mathcal{W}}}},\quad{\bm{w}}^{0}\in{\operatorname{vert}}\left({\mathcal{W}}\right),

(140)

{\bar{{\mathcal{W}}}}+\begin{bmatrix}{A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}+\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}{X^{\delta}}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{Y^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{\top}+\lambda{X^{\delta}}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&-\lambda\end{bmatrix}\preceq 0,{\bm{\zeta}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}.

(141)

In conclusion, by successfully solving (
139
), we can find matrices
{P^{\delta}}
and
{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}
such that incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
is given by (
133
) and satisfies (
44a
) and (
45
). Furthermore, the design of contraction LMI (
128
), RPI LMI (
138
), and Lipschitz LMI (
38
) and (
139f
) ensures satisfaction of (
44b
), (
44c
), and (
44d
). Therefore, we can conclude that this design satisfies Assumption
3
.

Terminal ingredients design

The terminal ingredients design involves computing a suitable terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})
such that Assumption
4
is satisfied. Given the design in the previous section, we know that
\sqrt{{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}}
in (
134
) decreases with at least factor
\rho
, or, equivalently,
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
decreases with at least factor
2\rho
, given control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}
in (
135
). Therefore, if we design the terminal control law as

{\kappa^{\mathrm{f}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{r}})}={\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}+{\kappa^{\delta}}({\bm{x}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})={\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}+\int_{0}^{1}{K^{\delta}({{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{x}}(s)})}ds\ ({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}),

(142)

we just need to come up with a matrix
P
such that the following descent bound based on (
54
) is satisfied:

\frac{d}{dt}\left(({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}})^{\top}P({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}})\right)\leq-{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{v}},{\bm{r}}),

(143)

with
{\dot{\bm{x}}}=f({\bm{x}},{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
and
{\dot{\bm{z}}}=f({{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}
. The following LMI ensures that this condition holds, see also
[
13
]
:

\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{\top}P+P\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)\preceq-Q-{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}^{\top}R{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})},\quad{\bm{\zeta}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}.

(144)

Thus, in order to find
P
with the lowest eigenvalues satisfying (
54
), we solve the following SDP:

\displaystyle\underset{P}{{\operatorname{min}}}\ \

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}P,

(145a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)^{\top}P+P\left({A({\bm{\zeta}})}+{B({\bm{\zeta}})}{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}\right)\preceq-Q-{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})}^{\top}R{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})},

(145b)

\displaystyle{\bm{\zeta}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}.

Remark 17
.

A simpler but more conservative solution would be to define constant scaling factor
\mu
such that
P=\mu{P^{\delta}}
. It can be shown that a lower bound for
\mu
can be formulated as a function of
Q,R,{K^{\delta}({\bm{x}})},\rho
, and
{c^{\delta\mathrm{,u}}}
such that the contraction of
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
by
2\rho
implies that the minimum terminal cost decrease in (
54
) is satisfied.

Thus, we can conclude that Assumption
4
is satisfied.

Reference trajectory design

Assumptions
5
and
6
are trivially satisfied if the system and obstacle avoidance constraints used for generating the reference trajectory are constructed according to (
83
) and (
84
) using tightening constants
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}},j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
and
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}
from SDP (
139
), respectively, and
\alpha
satisfying the lower bound computed in (
82
). Correspondingly, terminal set (
76
) is suitably designed around the reference trajectory to ensure closed-loop system and obstacle avoidance constraints satisfaction at all times.

4.4
Concluding remarks

In conclusion, the presented RMPC scheme is an effective tool to provide safety guarantees for tracking dynamically feasible reference trajectories with a mobile robot described by disturbed dynamics (
39
). The scheme is based on the idea that the impact of disturbances can be bounded by a sublevel set of incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
given a suitably designed control law. This bound, which is also called the tube size, can be used to tighten system and obstacle avoidance constraints such that the closed-loop system always satisfies the actual system and obstacle avoidance constraints. Recursive feasibility is proven by constructing a minimum invariant terminal set based on
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
. Furthermore, trajectory tracking is proven by showing that the terminal cost matrix
P
can be computed using the weighting matrix
{P^{\delta}}
used in
{V^{\delta}({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}}})}
.

Note that tightening the system and obstacle avoidance constraints of the reference trajectory by
{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}\alpha,j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}}
and
{c^{\mathrm{o}}}\alpha
, respectively, is a sufficient condition to guarantee closed-loop constraints satisfaction. However, a feasible solution is not required to start at least this far away from the constraints at the beginning of the prediction horizon since the constraints are tightened by an increasing tube size that starts from zero. Therefore, implementing a similar suitably designed constraint tightening scheme in the planner generating the reference trajectory will allow for less conservative motions, i.e., motions closer to obstacles, thereby reaching goals quicker.

In practice, a physical robot is subject not only to dynamic disturbances but also to noisy sensor measurements. This means that we cannot directly measure the states of the system. To provide safety guarantees in the context of sensor noise, one needs to identify the dynamical disturbances and sensor noise, compute suitable ingredients similar to the ones presented in this section, and implement a real-time RMPC scheme for which similar properties can be proven to the ones presented in this section.

5
Robust output-feedback MPC for trajectory tracking with disturbances

The goal of this section is to introduce a robust output-feedback MPC (ROMPC) scheme that will be used to track reference trajectories satisfying Property
1
when the system is described as given in Section
5.1
. In this system, the state can only be measured indirectly via noisy output measurements. Like Section
3.1
, Section
5.2
states the optimization problem and control law for the closed-loop system. Then, Section
5.3
builds an intuition for ROMPC and explains the required elements in the formulation and related properties. Finally, Section
5.4
gives concluding remarks on the ROMPC formulation.

5.1
Disturbed mobile robot with measurement noise description

The disturbed system dynamics are given by (
39
). The corresponding output equation is given by

{\bm{y}}=C{\bm{x}}+F{\bm{\eta}},

(146)

with measured output
{\bm{y}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{y}}}}}
, observation matrix
C\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{y}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{x}}}}}
, measurement or sensor noise
{\bm{\eta}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{\eta}}}}}
, and noise selection matrix
F\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{y}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{\eta}}}}}
.

The measurement noise is assumed to be contained in a known bounding box
{\bm{\eta}}\in H
. By recording data we can compute bounding box
H
with lower bound
{\underaccent{\bar}{\bmeta}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}
and upper bound
{\bar{{\bm{\eta}}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}
for each dimension
i\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{\eta}}}]}}
of the recorded measurement noise values:

H\coloneqq\left\{{\bm{\eta}}\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{\eta}}}}}\ \middle|\ {\underaccent{\bar}{\bmeta}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}}\leq{\bm{\eta}}_{i}\leq{\bar{{\bm{\eta}}}}_{i}^{\mathrm{rec}},\ i\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{\eta}}}]}}\right\}.

(147)

This measurement noise bounding box can be used in the ROMPC formulation described in the next section.

5.2
ROMPC formulation

Consider the following ROMPC formulation for tracking reference trajectory (
4
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{t}^{*}({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{r}}_{t})=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bm{z}}_{{\cdot|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\cdot|t}}\end{subarray}}{{\operatorname{min}}}\ \

\displaystyle\mathrlap{{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}},{\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}})+\int_{0}^{T}{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{r}}_{{t+\tau}})\ d\tau,}

(148a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s.t.}\

\displaystyle{\bm{z}}_{{0|t}}={\hat{\bm{x}}}_{t},

(148b)

\displaystyle s_{0}=0,

(148c)

\displaystyle{\dot{\bm{z}}}_{{\tau|t}}=f({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(148d)

\displaystyle{\dot{s}}_{\tau}=-\rho s_{\tau}+{\bar{w}^{\mathrm{o}}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(148e)

\displaystyle{g_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}})+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s}}}s_{\tau}+{c_{j}^{\mathrm{s,o}}}\epsilon\leq 0,

\displaystyle\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{s}}}]}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(148f)

\displaystyle{g_{j,{\tau|t}}^{\mathrm{o}}}(M{\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}})+{c^{\mathrm{o}}}(s_{\tau}+\epsilon)\leq 0,

\displaystyle\ j\in{\mathbb{N}_{[1,{n^{\mathrm{o}}}]}},

\displaystyle\ \tau\in[0,T],

(148g)

\displaystyle({\bm{z}}_{{T|t}},s_{\tau},\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{x}}_{{t+T}}^{\mathrm{r}}),

(148h)

with estimated state
{\hat{\bm{x}}}_{t}
, stage cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{s}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{v}},{\bm{r}})=\lVert{\bm{z}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{Q}^{2}+\lVert{\bm{v}}-{\bm{u}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{R}^{2},Q\succ 0,R\succ 0
and terminal cost
{{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{f}}}({\bm{z}},{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}})=\lVert{\bm{z}}-{\bm{x}^{\mathrm{r}}}\rVert_{P}^{2},P\succ 0
. In this formulation,
Q
and
R
are tuning matrices, and
P
should be suitably computed to show practical asymptotic convergence; see Section
5.3.3
.

Similar to the TMPC formulation in Section
3
, the ROMPC formulation is applied receding-horizon. In this case, the control law for the closed-loop system is defined as follows for
t\geq 0
:

{\bm{u}}_{{t+\tau}}=\kappa({\hat{\bm{x}}}_{{t+\tau}},{\bm{z}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*},{\bm{v}}_{{\tau|t}}^{*}),\ \tau\in[0,{T^{\mathrm{s}}}],

(149)

with control law
{\kappa({{\bm{x}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{v}}})}:{{{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{u}}}}}}
as defined in (
43
) that will be detailed later according to the design presented in this section.

5.3
ROMPC properties

The main difference between ROMPC and RMPC is that the actual states
{\bm{x}}
are hidden, and we can only measure noisy outputs
{\bm{y}}
. A logical choice in the ROMPC formulation is to predict a nominal trajectory starting at some estimated
{\hat{\bm{x}}}
based on
{\bm{y}}
instead of
{\bm{x}}
. This trajectory will satisfy tightened constraints (
148f
), (
148g
) and (
148h
). However, tightening these constraints with tube dynamics (
42e
) is not sufficient to guarantee satisfaction of the non-tightened constraints for the closed-loop trajectory since
{\bm{x}}
might be closer to the constraints than
{\bm{y}}
. Therefore, we want to increase the tightening to account for this additional observer error.

To determine the additional required tightening, the error
{\bm{y}}-C{\bm{x}}
needs to be bounded. This bound is given by the compact set
H
described in (
147
). Furthermore, as further detailed in Section
5.3.4
, we need to have an explicit expression for the derivative
\frac{d}{dt}({\bm{y}}-C{\bm{x}})
to determine the size of the tightening. Since this information is unavailable, we design an observer to estimate state
{\hat{\bm{x}}}
given output
{\bm{y}}
with the following dynamics:

{\dot{\hat{\bm{x}}}}\coloneqq f({\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{u}})+E{\bm{w}^{\mathrm{b}}}+L({\bm{y}}-C{\hat{\bm{x}}}),

(150)

with
{\bm{u}}
given in (
149
) and observer gain matrix
L\in{\mathbb{R}^{{n^{\mathrm{x}}}\times{n^{\mathrm{y}}}}}
, which serves as a low-pass filter on measurements
{\bm{y}}
.

Similar to the properties described in Section
4.3
and given observer (
150
), we want to design a feedback law
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{z}}})}
that renders tube size
s
of incremental Lyapunov function
{V^{\delta}({{\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{z}}})}
invariant. In this case,
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{z}}})}
and
{V^{\delta}({{\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{z}}})}
are based on a geodesic that ends at
{\hat{\bm{x}}}
rather than
{\bm{x}}
. Such a combination of
{{\kappa^{\delta}}({{\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{z}}})}
and
{V^{\delta}({{\hat{\bm{x}}},{\bm{z}}})}
exists if the system satisfies the following assumption:

Assumption 7
